A few more minutes of absolute silence, then something dark and furtive began to move out of the long grass which bordered the roadside﻿—something that in movement was almost like a snake. It dragged itself along close to the ground, making no sound as it moved. Soon it reached the hollow tree, rose to the height of a man and flattened itself against the tree-trunk. Then it put out a hand, felt for the hollow receptacle and groped for the missive which Henri de Montorgueil had dropped in there a while ago.

The next moment a tiny ray of light gleamed through the darkness like a star. A small, almost fragile, figure of a man, dressed in the mud-stained clothes of a country yokel, had turned up the shutter of a small lantern. By its flickering light he deciphered the letter which Henri de Montorgueil had written to Lucile Clamette.

===Chinese===

他用姓名缩写 H. de M. 署了名。随后他蹑手蹑脚折回大门，把信投进树洞里。

一刻钟后，亨利·德·蒙托尔格伊已踏上归途，回那处庇护了他们父子许久的藏身之所。空心树四周，寂静和黑暗重新无可争议地称了王。连细雨也停了它轻柔的滴答。不多时，远处传来教堂钟声，敲了十下。然后，万籁复归于无。

又是几分钟绝对的寂静。随后，路边深草丛里，有个黑黢黢、鬼鬼祟祟的东西开始蠕动——那动作几乎像一条蛇。它贴着地面匍匐前行，移动时不带一点声响。很快它到了空心树下，立起身来，一个男人的身量，紧贴树干站定。接着伸出一只手，摸到树洞，取出了亨利·德·蒙托尔格伊方才投进去的那封信。

转眼间，一线微光划破黑暗，亮得像一颗星。一个瘦小、近乎单薄的男人身影，穿着乡下泥点子衣裳，拨开了一盏小灯的门扉。借着摇曳的灯光，他辨认着亨利·德·蒙托尔格伊写给吕西尔·克拉梅特的那封信。


===Original===

“One day before the term you name I myself will place the papers there for you.”

A sigh of satisfaction, quickly suppressed, came through his thin, colourless lips, and the light of the lantern caught the flash of triumph in his pale, inscrutable eyes.

Then the light was extinguished. Impenetrable darkness swallowed up that slender, mysterious figure again.

===Chinese===

“在你指定的日子之前一天，我会亲自把文件放进去。”

一声满意的叹息，旋即压住，从他薄而无色的唇间逸出；灯光正好照见他淡色的、莫测的眼睛里一闪而过的得意。

随后灯光熄了。深不可测的黑暗，重新吞没了那瘦削而神秘的身影。


### V / ### 五

===Original===

Six days had gone by since Chauvelin had delivered his cruel “either﻿—or” to poor little Lucile Clamette; three since he had found Henri de Montorgueil’s reply to the girl’s appeal in the hollow of the tree. Since then he had made a careful investigation of the château, and soon was able to settle it in his own mind as to which room had been Madame la Marquise’s boudoir in the past. It was a small apartment, having direct access on the first landing of the staircase, and the one window gave on the rose garden at the back of the house. Inside the monumental hearth, at an arm’s length up the wide chimney, a receptacle had been contrived in the brickwork, with a small iron door which opened and closed with a secret spring. Chauvelin, whom his nefarious calling had rendered proficient in such matters, had soon mastered the workings of that spring. He could now open and close the iron door at will.

Up to a late hour on the sixth night of this weary waiting, the receptacle inside the chimney was still empty. That night Chauvelin had determined to spend at the château. He could not have rested elsewhere.

Even his colleague Lebel could not know what the possession of those papers would mean to the discredited agent of the Committee of Public Safety. With them in his hands, he could demand rehabilitation, and could purchase immunity from those sneers which had been so galling to his arrogant soul﻿—sneers which had become more and more marked, more and more unendurable, and more and more menacing, as he piled up failure on failure with every encounter with the Scarlet Pimpernel.

Immunity and rehabilitation! This would mean that he could once more measure his wits and his power with that audacious enemy who had brought about his downfall.

===Chinese===

自从肖维兰把那残酷的“要么——要么”摊给可怜的小吕西尔·克拉梅特，六天过去了；自从他在树洞里发现亨利·德·蒙托尔格伊对那姑娘哀告的答复，也过去了三天。这期间他把庄园里里外外仔细查勘了一遍，很快就认准了从前哪一间是侯爵夫人的闺房。那是间小屋子，正对楼梯的第一层平台有门直通，唯一一扇窗子朝向宅子后头的玫瑰园。那座气派的壁炉里，顺宽大的烟囱向上约一臂深处的砖壁上，设着一个暗格，装一扇小铁门，靠一个暗簧开合。肖维兰干他那伤天害理的行当，早练成了此道的行家，不多时便摸清了暗簧的机关。如今那扇铁门，他想开就开，想关就关。

到这焦心守候的第六夜深夜，烟囱里的暗格还空着。那一夜，肖维兰决意就在庄园里守着。换个地方，他安不下心来。

就连同僚勒贝尔，也未必明白那几份文件对公安委员会这个失宠暗探意味着什么。文件一到手，他便可要求恢复名誉，便可赎身免受那些讥诮——那讥诮刺痛了他倨傲的灵魂，而且随着他同红花侠一次又一次交手、一次失手叠着一次失手，越来越露骨，越来越难堪，也越来越杀气腾腾。

免罪与复宠！这就意味着，他可以再一次同那个断送他前程的胆大包天的仇人，较量智谋与实力。


===Original===

“In the name of Satan, bring us those papers!” Robespierre himself had cried with unwonted passion, ere he sent him out on this important mission. “We none of us could stand the scandal of such disclosures. It would mean absolute ruin for us all.”

And Chauvelin that night, as soon as the shades of evening had drawn in, took up his stand in the château, in the small inner room which was contiguous to the boudoir.

Here he sat, beside the open window, for hour upon hour, his every sense on the alert, listening for the first footfall upon the gravel path below. Though the hours went by leaden-footed, he was neither excited nor anxious. The Clamette family was such a precious hostage that the Montorgueils were bound to comply with Lucile’s demand for the papers by every dictate of honour and of humanity.

“While we have those people in our power,” Chauvelin had reiterated to himself more than once during the course of his long vigil, “even that meddlesome Scarlet Pimpernel can do nothing to save those cursed Montorgueils.”

===Chinese===

“以撒旦的名义，把那些文件给我们带回来！”派他出来办这桩要差之前，罗伯斯庇尔亲口疾言厉色地喊过，“这样的丑事一旦抖搂出来，咱们谁也招架不住。那意味着咱们大伙儿全完。”

于是那一夜，暮色刚一四合，肖维兰便到庄园里设下了埋伏，守在与闺房紧邻的那间小内室里。

他在敞开的窗边，一个钟头又一个钟头地坐着，浑身的感官都警醒着，捕捉下面砾石小径上的第一声脚步。时间虽然迈着铅一样沉的步子，他却既不亢奋，也不焦灼。克拉梅特一家是这样一件奇珍的人质，任凭道义还是人情，都逼着蒙托尔格伊父子非应允吕西尔索要文件的要求不可。

“只要这一家人攥在咱们手里，”漫漫守夜中，肖维兰不止一遍地对自己念叨，“哪怕那个爱管闲事的红花侠，也救不了那两个该死的蒙托尔格伊。”


===Original===

The night was dark and still. Not a breath of air stirred the branches of the trees or the shrubberies in the park; any footsteps, however wary, must echo through that perfect and absolute silence. Chauvelin’s keen, pale eyes tried to pierce the gloom in the direction whence in all probability the aristo would come. Vaguely he wondered if it would be Henri de Montorgueil or the old Marquis himself who would bring the papers.

“Bah! whichever one it is,” he muttered, “we can easily get the other, once those abominable papers are in our hands. And even if both the aristos escape,” he added mentally, “ ’tis no matter, once we have the papers.”

Anon, far away a distant church bell struck the midnight hour. The stillness of the air had become oppressive. A kind of torpor born of intense fatigue lulled the Terrorist’s senses to somnolence. His head fell forward on his breast.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

夜黑而静。一丝风也没有，园中的树枝树丛纹丝不动；任何脚步声，再怎么小心，也必定会撞破这份万籁无声。肖维兰那双锐利的淡色眼睛，竭力望穿贵族佬最可能来的那个方向的黑暗。他恍惚猜想着：来送文件的，会是亨利·德·蒙托尔格伊，还是老侯爵本人？

“呸！来的是哪一个都一样，”他喃喃自语，“等那几份可恶的文件一到手，再抓另一个还不容易。就算两个贵族佬都跑了，”他心里又补了一句，“也不要紧，只要文件到了手。”

不多时，远处一座教堂的钟敲了午夜十二点。凝滞的空气闷得人发慌。极度的疲惫生出一种昏沉，把这名恐怖分子的感官哄向了半梦半醒。他的头垂到了胸前。……


### VI / ### 六

===Original===

Then suddenly a shiver of excitement went right through him. He was fully awake now, with glowing eyes wide open and the icy calm of perfect confidence ruling every nerve. The sound of stealthy footsteps had reached his ear.

He could see nothing, either outside or in; but his fingers felt for the pistol which he carried in his belt. The aristo was evidently alone; only one solitary footstep was approaching the château.

Chauvelin had left the door ajar which gave on the boudoir. The staircase was on the other side of that fateful room, and the door leading to that was closed. A few minutes of tense expectancy went by. Then through the silence there came the sound of furtive footsteps on the stairs, the creaking of a loose board and finally the stealthy opening of the door.

===Chinese===

猛然间，一阵激奋的震颤流遍他全身。他霎时全醒了，眼睛睁得滚圆发亮，每一根神经都凝着稳操胜券的冰冷镇定。潜行的脚步声传进了他的耳朵。

屋外屋里，他什么也看不见；但他的手指摸上了插在腰间的手枪。贵族佬显然是独自来的；只有孤零零一个人的脚步，正朝庄园走来。

肖维兰早把通闺房的那扇门虚掩着。楼梯在那间祸福难料的屋子另一侧，通楼梯的门则关着。几分钟的紧张等待过去。随后，寂静中传来楼梯上潜行的脚步声，一块松动的地板吱呀一响，最后，是那扇门被悄悄推开。


===Original===

In all his adventurous career Chauvelin had never felt so calm. His heart beat quite evenly, his senses were undisturbed by the slightest tingling of his nerves. The stealthy sounds in the next room brought the movements of the aristo perfectly clear before his mental vision. The latter was carrying a small dark lantern. As soon as he entered he flashed its light about the room. Then he deposited the lantern on the floor, close beside the hearth, and started to feel up the chimney for the hidden receptacle.

Chauvelin watched him now like a cat watches a mouse, savouring these few moments of anticipated triumph. He pushed open the door noiselessly which gave on the boudoir. By the feeble light of the lantern on the ground he could only see the vague outline of the aristo’s back, bending forward to his task; but a thrill went through him as he saw a bundle of papers lying on the ground close by.

Everything was ready; the trap was set. Here was a complete victory at last. It was obviously the young Vicomte de Montorgueil who had come to do the deed. His head was up the chimney even now. The old Marquis’s back would have looked narrower and more fragile. Chauvelin held his breath; then he gave a sharp little cough, and took the pistol from his belt.

===Chinese===

肖维兰一生冒险生涯里，从不曾这样镇定过。他心跳得平平稳稳，感官里没有一丝神经的震颤。隔壁屋里的窸窣声响，把贵族佬的一举一动清清楚楚映在他的心眼里。来人提着一盏小暗灯。一进门，他便拿灯光在屋里扫了一圈，然后把灯搁在紧挨壁炉的地上，伸手往烟囱里摸那个暗藏的格子。

此刻肖维兰盯着他，像猫盯老鼠，咂摸着这几秒钟唾手可得的胜利的滋味。他悄没声地推开通闺房的门。借着地上那盏灯的微光，他只能看见贵族佬背部的模糊轮廓，正俯身忙着他的事；可是一眼瞥见近旁地上放着一扎文件，他浑身掠过一阵战栗。

万事俱备；陷阱已经张好。到头来这是一场十全十美的胜利。来干这活的，显然是年轻的蒙托尔格伊子爵。这会儿他的头正伸在烟囱里呢。若是老侯爵，背影该显得瘦窄单薄些。肖维兰屏住呼吸；随后短促地咳了一声，从腰带上拔出手枪。


===Original===

The sound caused the aristo to turn, and the next moment a loud and merry laugh roused the dormant echoes of the old château, whilst a pleasant, drawly voice said in English:

“I am demmed if this is not my dear old friend M. Chambertin! Zounds, sir! who’d have thought of meeting you here?”

Had a cannon suddenly exploded at Chauvelin’s feet he would, I think, have felt less unnerved. For the space of two heartbeats he stood there, rooted to the spot, his eyes glued on his arch-enemy, that execrated Scarlet Pimpernel, whose mocking glance, even through the intervening gloom, seemed to have deprived him of consciousness. But that phase of helplessness only lasted for a moment; the next, all the marvellous possibilities of this encounter flashed through the Terrorist’s keen mind.

===Chinese===

这一声咳叫贵族佬转过身来。转眼间，一阵响亮欢畅的笑声惊醒了古老庄园里沉睡的回声，一个悦耳的、拖长调子的嗓音用英语说道：

“这不是我的老朋友尚贝尔坦先生（M. Chambertin）吗，真他妈巧了！好家伙，先生！谁想得到在这儿碰上您呐？”

我想，纵然一门大炮突然在肖维兰脚边炸响，也不至于叫他这样魂飞魄散。足有两下心跳的工夫，他钉在原地，眼睛死死盯着他的死敌——那个千咒万骂的红花侠——那道讥诮的目光，纵然隔着中间的黑暗，也仿佛夺走了他的知觉。可这失魂落魄的架势只撑了一瞬；下一瞬，这次会面所蕴含的一切妙不可言的机会，已闪过这名恐怖分子敏锐的头脑。


===Original===

Everything was ready; the trap was set! The unfortunate Clamettes were still the bait which now would bring a far more noble quarry into the mesh than even he﻿—Chauvelin﻿—had dared to hope.

He raised his pistol, ready to fire. But already Sir Percy Blakeney was on him, and with a swift movement, which the other was too weak to resist, he wrenched the weapon from his enemy’s grasp.

“Why, how hasty you are, my dear M. Chambertin,” he said lightly. “Surely you are not in such a hurry to put a demmed bullet into me!”

===Chinese===

万事俱备；陷阱已经张好！不幸的克拉梅特一家仍是那块诱饵，如今要引进网里的，是一份远比他——肖维兰——敢于奢望的更高贵的猎物。

他举枪待发。可珀西·布莱克尼爵士已经扑到他跟前，一个迅捷的动作——对方虚弱得无从抗拒——就把武器从敌人手里夺了下来。

“哎呀，您性子多急呀，我亲爱的尚贝尔坦先生，”他轻飘飘地说，“您总不至于急着往我身上塞一颗该死的子弹吧！”


===Original===

The position now was one which would have made even a braver man than Chauvelin quake. He stood alone and unarmed in face of an enemy from whom he could expect no mercy. But, even so, his first thought was not of escape. He had not only apprised his own danger, but also the immense power which he held whilst the Clamettes remained as hostages in the hands of his colleague Lebel.

“You have me at a disadvantage, Sir Percy,” he said, speaking every whit as coolly as his foe. “But only momentarily. You can kill me, of course; but if I do not return from this expedition not only safe and sound, but with a certain packet of papers in my hands, my colleague Lebel has instructions to proceed at once against the girl Clamette and the whole family.”

“I know that well enough,” rejoined Sir Percy with a quaint laugh. “I know what venomous reptiles you and those of your kidney are. You certainly do owe your life at the present moment to the unfortunate girl whom you are persecuting with such infamous callousness.”

===Chinese===

眼下的处境，换了比肖维兰更胆壮的人也要发抖。他孤身一人，手无寸铁，面对着一个休想指望怜悯的敌人。可即便如此，他的第一个念头也不是逃。他掂量的不光是自身的危险，还有克拉梅特一家作为人质攥在同僚勒贝尔手里时，自己所握有的那份巨大的权力。

“您这回是占了我的上风，珀西爵士，”他说道，口气的冷静与对手分毫不让，“不过只此一刻。您当然可以杀了我；可要是我不但没能安然无恙地回去，还没能把那一扎文件带回手上，我的同僚勒贝尔领有指令，立刻对克拉梅特丫头和她全家动手。”

“这个我清楚得很。”珀西爵士答道，发出一声古怪的笑，“我知道您和您这路人都是怎样的毒虫。您眼下这条命，确实该归功于那个正被您这样无耻地冷酷迫害的不幸姑娘。”


===Original===

Chauvelin drew a sigh of relief. The situation was shaping itself more to his satisfaction already. Through the gloom he could vaguely discern the Englishman’s massive form standing a few paces away, one hand buried in his breeches pockets, the other still holding the pistol. On the ground close by the hearth was the small lantern, and in its dim light the packet of papers gleamed white and tempting in the darkness. Chauvelin’s keen eyes had fastened on it, saw the form of receipt for money with Hériot’s signature, which he recognised, on the top.

He himself had never felt so calm. The only thing he could regret was that he was alone. Half a dozen men now, and this impudent foe could indeed be brought to his knees. And this time there would be no risks taken, no chances for escape. Somehow it seemed to Chauvelin as if something of the Scarlet Pimpernel’s audacity and foresight had gone from him. As he stood there, looking broad and physically powerful, there was something wavering and undecided in his attitude, as if the edge had been taken off his former recklessness and enthusiasm. He had brought the compromising papers here, had no doubt helped the Montorgueils to escape; but while Lucile Clamette and her family were under the eye of Lebel no amount of impudence could force a successful bargaining.

It was Chauvelin now who appeared the more keen and the more alert; the Englishman seemed undecided what to do next, remained silent, toying with the pistol. He even smothered a yawn. Chauvelin saw his opportunity. With the quick movement of a cat pouncing upon a mouse he stooped and seized that packet of papers, would then and there have made a dash for the door with them, only that, as he seized the packet, the string which held it together gave way and the papers were scattered all over the floor.

===Chinese===

肖维兰宽慰地舒了口气。局面已经开始朝他如意的方向展开了。透过黑暗，他隐约辨出英国人魁伟的身躯立在几步开外，一只手插在马裤口袋里，另一只手还握着手枪。壁炉近旁的地上是那盏小灯，昏黄灯光里，那扎文件在黑暗中泛着白光，诱人极了。肖维兰锐利的眼睛盯住了它，看清最上面那一张是一纸银钱收据，签着埃里奥的名字——那个签名他认得。

他自己从不曾这样镇定。唯一遗憾的是孤身一人。眼下若有半打人手，这个放肆的仇敌当真会被打跪在地。而这一回不会再冒半点风险，不再给他半点逃脱的机会。不知怎么，肖维兰只觉得红花侠身上那股胆气与先见之明仿佛不见了。他站在那里，身板宽阔，体力雄健，姿态里却透着几分摇摆不定、迟疑不决，仿佛昔日那股不管不顾的劲头和热望钝了刃。他把那扎牵连朝政的文件带到了这里，无疑也帮蒙托尔格伊父子逃了出去；可只要吕西尔·克拉梅特一家还在勒贝尔眼皮底下，任凭怎样的放肆，也讨不成一场划算的交易。

此刻显得更热切、更警觉的反倒是肖维兰；英国人似乎拿不定主意下一步怎么办，一言不发，只把玩着手枪，甚至还掩着嘴打了个呵欠。肖维兰觑准了机会。他以猫扑老鼠般的迅捷俯身抄起那扎文件，本想当场就夺门而出，偏偏一把抓起来时，捆文件的绳子断了，文件撒了一地。


===Original===

Receipts for money? Compromising letters? No! Blank sheets of paper, all of them﻿—all except the one which had lain tantalisingly on the top: the one receipt signed by citizen Hériot. Sir Percy laughed lightly:

“Did you really think, my good friend,” he said, “that I would be such a demmed fool as to place my best weapon so readily to your hand?”

“Your best weapon, Sir Percy!” retorted Chauvelin, with a sneer. “What use is it to you while we hold Lucile Clamette?”

===Chinese===

银钱收据？牵连的信件？不！全是白纸，一张张全是——只有最上面那张逗弄人的例外：唯一一张签着埃里奥公民名字的收据。珀西爵士轻轻笑了：

“我亲爱的朋友，您当真以为，”他说，“我会蠢得那样该死，把自己最好的武器这么现成地送到您手边吗？”

“您最好的武器，珀西爵士！”肖维兰冷笑着回敬，“只要吕西尔·克拉梅特在咱们手里，它对您还有什么用？”


===Original===

“While I hold Lucile Clamette, you mean, my dear Monsieur Chambertin,” riposted Blakeney with elaborate blandness.

“You hold Lucile Clamette? Bah! I defy you to drag a whole family like that out of our clutches. The man a cripple, the children helpless! And you think they can escape our vigilance when all our men are warned! How do you think they are going to get across the river, Sir Percy, when every bridge is closely watched? How will they get across Paris, when at every gate our men are on the lookout for them?”

“They can’t do it, my dear Monsieur Chambertin,” rejoined Sir Percy blandly, “else I were not here.”

===Chinese===

“您是想说，只要吕西尔·克拉梅特在我手里吧，我亲爱的尚贝尔坦先生。”布莱克尼装出十足的和气，反唇相讥。

“您手里攥着吕西尔·克拉梅特？呸！谅您也没本事把这样一大家子人从咱们的爪子里拖出去。男人是个瘫子，孩子们什么都不懂！您还当咱们的人全得了警报，他们逃得出咱们的眼线！您倒想想，珀西爵士，座座桥都盯得死死的，他们怎么过河？道道城门都有咱们的人守着他们，他们怎么穿出巴黎城？”

“他们是办不到的，我亲爱的尚贝尔坦先生，”珀西爵士和颜悦色地答道，“要不然，我也就不会在这儿了。”


===Original===

Then, as Chauvelin, fuming, irritated despite himself, as he always was when he encountered that impudent Englishman, shrugged his shoulders in token of contempt, Blakeney’s powerful grasp suddenly clutched his arm.

“Let us understand one another, my good M. Chambertin,” he said coolly. “Those unfortunate Clamettes, as you say, are too helpless and too numerous to smuggle across Paris with any chance of success. Therefore I look to you to take them under your protection. They are all stowed away comfortably at this moment in a conveyance which I have provided for them. That conveyance is waiting at the bridgehead now. We could not cross without your help; we could not get across Paris without your august presence and your tricolour scarf of office. So you are coming with us, my dear M. Chambertin,” he continued, and, with force which was quite irresistible, he began to drag his enemy after him towards the door. “You are going to sit in that conveyance with the Clamettes, and I myself will have the honour to drive you. And at every bridgehead you will show your pleasing countenance and your scarf of office to the guard and demand free passage for yourself and your family, as a representative member of the Committee of Public Safety. And then we’ll enter Paris by the Porte d’Ivry and leave it by the Batignolles; and everywhere your charming presence will lull the guards’ suspicions to rest. I pray you, come! There is no time to consider! At noon tomorrow, without a moment’s grace, my friend Sir Andrew Ffoulkes, who has the papers in his possession, will dispose of them as he thinks best unless I myself do claim them from him.”

While he spoke he continued to drag his enemy along with him, with an assurance and an impudence which were past belief. Chauvelin was trying to collect his thoughts; a whirl of conflicting plans were running riot in his mind. The Scarlet Pimpernel in his power! At any point on the road he could deliver him up to the nearest guard﻿ ﻿… then still hold the Clamettes and demand the papers.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

肖维兰只要撞上这个放肆的英国人，便总要恼火，压也压不住；此刻他耸了耸肩，满是不屑，不想布莱克尼有力的铁掌忽然攥住了他的胳膊。

“咱们把话说透，我的好尚贝尔坦先生，”他冷冷地说，“正如您所说，那不幸的克拉梅特一家，人这样无助，人手又这样多，要偷运过巴黎城，成功的机会微乎其微。所以，我把他们托付给您保护了。此刻他们全都安安稳稳待在我给他们备下的一辆车里。那辆车正在桥头候着。没有您相助，我们过不了桥；没有您大驾亲临、没有您那条三色绶带，我们出不了巴黎城。所以您这就跟我们一道走，我亲爱的尚贝尔坦先生，”他接着说，以一种全然无法抗拒的力气，拽着他的仇人往门口拖，“您这就去同克拉梅特一家坐在那辆车里，我本人有幸给您赶车。每到一座桥头，您就把您那讨人喜欢的尊容和您的绶带亮给哨兵看，以公安委员会委员的身价，为您本人和您的家眷讨一道通行。然后咱们打伊夫里门（Porte d’Ivry）进巴黎，打巴蒂尼奥勒（Batignolles）出去；一路上有您大人在，处处都能哄得哨兵疑心尽消。求您了，来吧！没工夫斟酌了！明天正午，宽限一刻也没有，我的朋友安德鲁·福克斯爵士就会按他自己认为最妥的法子处置那些文件——除非我本人向他去讨。”

他一路说着，一路拽着他的仇人往前走，那份笃定和放肆，简直匪夷所思。肖维兰竭力收拢思绪；一团乱麻似的互相打架的计划，在他脑子里横冲直撞。红花侠在他手心里了！大路上随便哪一点，他都可以把这个人交给最近的哨所……回过头再攥着克拉梅特一家，讨要那些文件。……


===Original===

“Too late, my dear Monsieur Chambertin!” Sir Percy’s mocking voice broke in, as if divining his thoughts. “You do not know where to find my friend Ffoulkes, and at noon tomorrow, if I do not arrive to claim those papers, there will not be a single ragamuffin in Paris who will not be crying your shame and that of your precious colleagues upon the housetops.”

Chauvelin’s whole nervous system was writhing with the feeling of impotence. Mechanically, unresisting now, he followed his enemy down the main staircase of the château and out through the wide open gates. He could not bring himself to believe that he had been so completely foiled, that this impudent adventurer had him once more in the hollow of his hand.

“In the name of Satan, bring us back those papers!” Robespierre had commanded. And now he﻿—Chauvelin﻿—was left in a maze of doubt; and the vital alternative was hammering in his brain: “The Scarlet Pimpernel﻿—or those papers﻿—” Which, in Satan’s name, was the more important? Passion whispered “The Scarlet Pimpernel!” but common sense and the future of his party, the whole future of the Revolution mayhap, demanded those compromising papers. And all the while he followed that relentless enemy through the avenues of the park and down the lonely lane. Overhead the trees of the forest of Sucy, nodding in a gentle breeze, seemed to mock his perplexity.

===Chinese===

“来不及了，我亲爱的尚贝尔坦先生！”珀西爵士讥诮的声音插进来，仿佛看穿了他的心思，“您不知道上哪儿找我的朋友福克斯；而明天正午，我若不去讨那些文件，巴黎城里每一个小瘪三都会爬上房顶，把您的丑事、还有您那些宝贝同僚的丑事，喊得满城风雨。”

肖维兰全副神经都因这份无能为力而扭曲抽搐。此刻他机械地、不再反抗地跟着仇人走下庄园的大楼梯，出了敞开的大门。他无法叫自己相信，他竟被挫败得这样干净彻底，这个放肆的冒险家又把他玩弄于股掌之上。

“以撒旦的名义，把那些文件给我们带回来！”这是罗伯斯庇尔的命令。而如今他——肖维兰——被撇进了一座疑虑的迷宫；那个性命攸关的抉择在他脑中咚咚锤打：“红花侠——还是那些文件——”看在撒旦份上，哪一样更要紧？激情报耳低语：“红花侠！”可理智、他那一党的前途、兴许还有整个革命的前途，都索要那些牵连朝政的文件。他就这样一路跟着那不留情面的仇人，穿过庄园的林荫道，走下冷清的窄巷。头顶上，絮西森林（forest of Sucy）的树梢在微风中摇曳，仿佛在嗤笑他的左右为难。


===Original===

He had not arrived at a definite decision when the river came in sight, and when anon a carriage lantern threw a shaft of dim light through the mist-laden air. Now he felt as if he were in a dream. He was thrust unresisting into a closed chaise, wherein he felt the presence of several other people﻿—children, an old man who was muttering ceaselessly. As in a dream he answered questions at the bridge to a guard whom he knew well.

“You know me﻿—Armand Chauvelin, of the Committee of Public Safety!”

As in a dream, he heard the curt words of command:

===Chinese===

河望见了影子、一盏车灯的光柱穿过雾气沉沉的夜色投过来时，他都没能拿定一个明确的主意。这时他觉得恍如梦中。他不作抵抗，被塞进一辆带篷的马车，车里觉出还坐着好几个人——孩子们，还有一个喃喃呓语不停的老头。恍如梦中，他在桥头答着问话，盘问的哨兵是他熟识的。

“你认得我——公安委员会的阿尔芒·肖维兰（Armand Chauvelin）！”

恍如梦中，他听见那简短的口令：


===Original===

“Pass on, in the name of the Republic!”

And all the while the thought hammered in his brain: “Something must be done! This is impossible! This cannot be! It is not I﻿—Chauvelin﻿—who am sitting here, helpless, unresisting. It is not that impudent Scarlet Pimpernel who is sitting there before me on the box, driving me to utter humiliation!”

And yet it was all true. All real. The Clamette children were sitting in front of him, clinging to Lucile, terrified of him even now. The old man was beside him﻿—imbecile and not understanding. The boy Etienne was up on the box next to that audacious adventurer, whose broad back appeared to Chauvelin like a rock on which all his hopes and dreams must forever be shattered.

===Chinese===

“放行，以共和国的名义！”

而这一路，那个念头始终在他脑子里咚咚锤打：“得想个法子！这不可能！这不能是真的！坐在这里束手无策、任人摆布的，不是我——不是肖维兰！坐在前头车板上、把我赶向奇耻大辱的，也不是那个放肆的红花侠！”

然而这一切都是真的。全是真的。克拉梅特的孩子们坐在他对面，偎着吕西尔，到这时还怕他。老头坐在他身旁——痴痴呆呆，什么都不明白。少年艾蒂安坐在车板上，紧挨着那个胆大包天的冒险家；那宽阔的背影，在肖维兰眼里像一块岩石，他所有的希望和迷梦，注定要永远撞碎在上面。


===Original===

The chaise rattled triumphantly through the Batignolles. It was then broad daylight. A brilliant early autumn day after the rains. The sun, the keen air, all mocked Chauvelin’s helplessness, his humiliation. Long before noon they passed St. Denis. Here the barouche turned off the main road, halted at a small wayside house﻿—nothing more than a cottage. After which everything seemed more dreamlike than ever. All that Chauvelin remembered of it afterwards was that he was once more alone in a room with his enemy, who had demanded his signature to a number of safe-conducts, ere he finally handed over the packet of papers to him.

“How do I know that they are all here?” he heard himself vaguely muttering, while his trembling fingers handled that precious packet.

“That’s just it!” his tormentor retorted airily. “You don’t know. I don’t know myself,” he added, with a light laugh. “And, personally, I don’t see how either of us can possibly ascertain. In the meanwhile, I must bid you au revoir, my dear M. Chambertin. I am sorry that I cannot provide you with a conveyance, and you will have to walk a league or more ere you meet one, I fear me. We, in the meanwhile, will be well on our way to Dieppe, where my yacht, the *Daydream*, lies at anchor, and I do not think that it will be worth your while to try and overtake us. I thank you for the safe-conducts. They will make our journey exceedingly pleasant. Shall I give your regards to M. le Marquis de Montorgueil or to M. le Vicomte? They are on board the *Daydream*, you know. Oh! and I was forgetting! Lady Blakeney desired to be remembered to you.”

===Chinese===

马车一路威风凛凛，辘辘驶过巴蒂尼奥勒。这时已是朗朗白昼。一场秋雨过后晴朗的初秋天气。太阳、清冽的空气，都在奚落肖维兰的无能为力、奚落他的屈辱。还不到晌午，他们过了圣德尼（St. Denis）。到这里，马车拐下大路，停在一座路旁的小屋前——不过是间村舍。此后的一切，比先前更像梦境了。事后肖维兰记得起的，只有他又和仇人单独待在一间屋里，那人在把那扎文件最终交到他手里之前，要去了他签好的一叠通行证。

“我怎么知道文件都在这儿？”他听见自己恍恍惚惚地咕哝，哆嗦的手指摆弄着那扎宝贝文件。

“问到点子上了！”折磨他的人轻飘飘地回敬，“您不知道。我自己也不知道，”他又轻笑一声，补了句，“而且说句私话，我看咱们俩谁也没法查明。此刻，我得向您道一声 au revoir（再会）了，我亲爱的尚贝尔坦先生。很抱歉，我不能给您备车，我担心您得走上一里格还多的路，才遇得着一辆。我们嘛，这就要顺顺当当上迪耶普（Dieppe）去了，我的游艇“白日梦”号（*Daydream*）在那儿下锚候着，我想您还是犯不上追我们。多谢您的通行证。有它一路，咱们的行程定会愉快至极。要我替您向蒙托尔格伊侯爵先生或是子爵先生问好吗？他们就在“白日梦”号上，您知道。哦！差点忘了！布莱克尼夫人（Lady Blakeney）向您致意。”


===Original===

The next moment he was gone. Chauvelin, standing at the window of the wayside house, saw Sir Percy Blakeney once more mount the box of the chaise. This time he had Sir Andrew Ffoulkes beside him. The Clamette family were huddled together﻿—happy and free﻿—inside the vehicle. After which there was the usual clatter of horses’ hoofs, the creaking of wheels, the rattle of chains. Chauvelin saw and heard nothing of that. All that he saw at the last was Sir Percy’s slender hand, waving him a last adieu.

After which he was left alone with his thoughts. The packet of papers was in his hand. He fingered it, felt its crispness, clutched it with a fierce gesture, which was followed by a long-drawn-out sigh of intense bitterness.

No one would ever know what it had cost him to obtain these papers. No one would ever know how much he had sacrificed of pride, revenge and hate in order to save a few shreds of his own party’s honour.

===Chinese===

转眼间人已没了影。肖维兰站在路旁小屋的窗前，望着珀西·布莱克尼爵士重新登上车板。这一回，他身边坐着安德鲁·福克斯爵士。克拉梅特一家挤在车里——快活，自由。随后照例是一阵马蹄得得、车轮吱呀、链子哗啦。这些肖维兰既没看见，也没听见。他最后看见的，只是珀西爵士纤瘦的手，朝他挥了挥，作最后的道别。

此后，只剩他独自守着一腔心思。那扎文件在他手里。他用手指捻着它，摸着它的挺括，狠狠一把攥紧，随后是一声悠长的、苦涩之极的叹息。

谁也不会知道，为弄到这几份文件，他付出了怎样的代价。谁也不会知道，为了保全他那一党名誉的几块碎片，他牺牲了多少骄傲、多少复仇、多少仇恨。

## Out of the Jaws of Death / ## 脱离死亡之口

===Original===
Being a fragment from the diary of Valentine Lemercier, in the possession of her great-granddaughter.

We were such a happy family before this terrible Revolution broke out; we lived rather simply, but very comfortably, in our dear old home just on the borders of the forest of Compiegne. Jean and André were the twins; just fifteen years old they were when King Louis was deposed from the throne of France which God had given him, and sent to prison like a common criminal, with our beautiful Queen Marie Antoinette and the Royal children, and Madame Elizabeth, who was so beloved by the poor!

Ah! that seems very, very long ago now. No doubt you know better than I do all that happened in our beautiful land of France and in lovely Paris about that time: goods and property confiscated, innocent men, women, and children condemned to death for acts of treason which they had never committed.

===Chinese===
本篇系瓦朗蒂娜·勒梅西耶（Valentine Lemercier）日记中的一页残章，如今由她的曾孙女珍藏着。

在这场可怕的革命爆发之前，我们原是一个多么幸福的家庭啊；我们住在贡比涅（Compiegne）森林边上那栋亲爱的老宅里，度日虽算简朴，却十分安逸。让（Jean）和安德烈（André）是一对双胞胎兄弟；路易国王（King Louis）被废黜了上帝赐予他的法兰西王位，同我们美丽的王后玛丽·安托瓦内特（Marie Antoinette）、王室的孩子，以及深得穷人爱戴的伊丽莎白夫人（Madame Elizabeth）一道，像寻常罪犯似的被投进了监狱——那时他们俩刚满十五岁！

啊！如今想来，那已是极其遥远的往事了。无疑，那时在我们美丽的法兰西国土上、在可爱的巴黎城里所发生的一切，你比我知道得更清楚：财物家产横遭抄没，无辜的男女乃至孩童，因从未犯下的叛国罪名被判处死刑。

===Original===
It was in August last year that they came to Mon Repos and arrested papa, and maman, and us four young ones and dragged us to Paris, where we were imprisoned in a narrow and horribly dank vault in the Abbaye, where all day and night through the humid stone walls we heard cries and sobs and moans from poor people, who no doubt were suffering the same sorrows and the same indignities as we were.

I had just passed my nineteenth birthday, and Marguerite was only thirteen. Maman was a perfect angel during that terrible time; she kept up our courage and our faith in God in a way that no one else could have done. Every night and morning we knelt round her knee and papa sat close beside her, and we prayed to God for deliverance from our own afflictions, and for the poor people who were crying and moaning all the day.

But of what went on outside our prison walls we had not an idea, though sometimes poor papa would brave the warder’s brutalities and ask him questions of what was happening in Paris every day.

===Chinese===
去年八月，他们来到蒙勒波（Mon Repos），逮捕了爸爸、妈妈和我们四个孩子，把我们拖到巴黎，关进阿贝伊（Abbaye）监狱里一间狭窄而潮气刺骨的地窖式牢房。日日夜夜，透过湿漉漉的石墙，我们听得见可怜人的哭喊、呜咽与呻吟——他们无疑正遭受着与我们相同的苦难和屈辱。

我刚过完十九岁生日，玛格丽特（Marguerite）才十三岁。在那段可怕的日子里，妈妈真是一位十足的天使；她以任何旁人都无法做到的方式，支撑着我们的勇气和对上帝的信心。每天清晨和夜晚，我们跪坐在她的膝旁，爸爸紧挨着她，我们一起祈求上帝把我们从自身的苦难中解救出来，也垂怜那些终日啼哭呻吟的可怜人。

至于高墙之外发生的事，我们一无所知；尽管有时可怜的爸爸会壮起胆子，不顾狱卒的凶横，向他打听巴黎每日的动静。

===Original===
“They are hanging all the aristos to the streetlamps of the city,” the man would reply with a cruel laugh, “and it will be your turn next.”

We had been in prison for about a fortnight, when one day﻿—oh! shall I ever forget it?﻿—we heard in the distance a noise like the rumbling of thunder; nearer and nearer it came, and soon the sound became less confused, cries and shrieks could be heard above that rumbling din; but so weird and menacing did those cries seem that instinctively﻿—though none of us knew what they meant﻿—we all felt a nameless terror grip our hearts.

Oh! I am not going to attempt the awful task of describing to you all the horrors of that never-to-be-forgotten day. People, who today cannot speak without a shudder of the September massacres, have not the remotest conception of what really happened on that awful second day of that month.

===Chinese===
“他们正把贵族佬一个个吊上城里的路灯哪，”那人会带着残忍的狞笑答道，“很快就轮到你们了。”

我们入狱大约两个星期后，有一天——哦！我哪能忘得了那一天？——我们听见远处传来一阵闷雷滚动般的轰响；它越来越近，很快那声响便不再混沌，在隆隆的喧嚣之上，听得出一声声呼叫与尖啸；那些喊声听来如此怪诞而凶险，我们虽谁也不明白它们意味着什么，却都不由自主地感到一种无名的恐怖攥住了心。

哦！我并不打算尝试向你描述那个永志难忘的日子里全部的恐怖——那桩差事太可怕了。如今一提起九月大屠杀（September massacres）仍不免战栗的人们，对那个月可怕的第二天究竟发生了什么，实在连最朦胧的概念也没有。

===Original===
We are all at peace and happy now, but whenever my thoughts fly back to that morning, whenever the ears of memory recall those hideous yells of fury and of hate, coupled with the equally horrible cries for pity, which pierced through the walls behind which the six of us were crouching, trembling, and praying, whenever I think of it all my heart still beats violently with that same nameless dread which held it in its deathly grip then.

Hundreds of men, women, and children were massacred in the prisons of that day﻿—it was a St. Bartholomew even more hideous than the last.

Maman was trying in vain to keep our thoughts fixed upon God﻿—papa sat on the stone bench, his elbows resting on his knees, his head buried in his hands; but maman was kneeling on the floor, with her dear arms encircling us all and her trembling lips moving in continuous prayer.

We felt that we were facing death﻿—and what a death, O my God!

===Chinese===
如今我们全都安居无恙、幸福快乐，可每当我的思绪飞回那个早晨，每当记忆的耳畔重新响起那一片狂怒与仇恨的狰狞吼叫，连同那同样骇人的哀哀乞怜之声——它们穿透墙壁，刺向我们六个蜷缩着、颤抖着、祈祷着的人——每当想起这一切，我的心至今仍会狂跳不已，仍被当年那种攥紧了它的无名恐惧死死扼住。

数以百计的男人、女人和儿童，在那一天各所监狱里惨遭屠戮——那是一场比当年更狰狞的圣巴托罗缪大屠杀（St. Bartholomew）。

妈妈徒劳地想把我们的心思系在上帝身上——爸爸坐在石凳上，两肘撑膝，头埋在双手里；妈妈则跪在地上，用她亲爱的臂膀把我们全都搂着，颤抖的双唇不住地翕动祷告。

我们觉得死亡就在眼前——那是怎样的一个死法啊，我的上帝！

===Original===
Suddenly the small grated window﻿—high up in the dank wall﻿—became obscured. I was the first to look up, but the cry of terror which rose from my heart was choked ere it reached my throat.

Jean and André looked up, too, and they shrieked, and so did Marguerite, and papa jumped up and ran to us and stood suddenly between us and the window like a tiger defending its young.

But we were all of us quite silent now. The children did not even cry; they stared, wide-eyed, paralysed with fear.

Only maman continued to pray, and we could hear papa’s rapid and stertorous breathing as he watched what was going on at that window above.

Heavy blows were falling against the masonry round the grating, and we could hear the nerve-racking sound of a file working on the iron bars; and farther away, below the window, those awful yells of human beings transformed by hate and fury into savage beasts.

===Chinese===
忽然，那扇小小的装着铁栅的窗——开在潮湿石墙的高处——被什么东西遮暗了。我第一个抬起头，可那声自心底涌起的恐怖惊呼，还没到喉咙便噎住了。

让和安德烈也抬起头来，尖声叫出了声，玛格丽特也是；爸爸一跃而起，奔到我们身边，猛地挡在我们与那扇窗之间，像一头护崽的猛虎。

但此刻我们全都鸦雀无声。孩子们连哭都忘了，只是瞪大眼睛呆望着，吓得僵住了。

只有妈妈仍在祷告；爸爸紧盯着上方那扇窗的动静，我们听得见他急促而粗重的呼吸。

沉重的击打一下一下落在铁栅四周的砖石上，我们听得出锉刀啃咬铁栏那令人神经绷裂的声响；而在更远处、窗子的下方，是那一片骇人的咆哮——那是被仇恨与狂怒变成了野兽的人。

===Original===
How long this horrible suspense lasted I cannot now tell you; the next thing I remember clearly is a number of men in horrible ragged clothing pouring into our vault-like prison from the window above; the next moment they rushed at us simultaneously﻿—or so it seemed to me, for I was just then recommending my soul to God, so certain was I that in that same second I would cease to live.

It was all like a dream, for instead of the horrible shriek of satisfied hate which we were all expecting to hear, a whispering voice, commanding and low, struck our ears and dragged us, as it were, from out the abyss of despair into the sudden light of hope.

“If you will trust us,” the voice whispered, “and not be afraid, you will be safely out of Paris within an hour.”

Papa was the first to realise what was happening; he had never lost his presence of mind even during the darkest moment of this terrible time, and he said quite calmly and steadily now:

===Chinese===
这番可怕的煎熬持续了多久，如今我说不上来了；我记得清楚的下一件事，是几个衣衫褴褛得吓人的男人，从上方那扇窗涌进了我们这间地窖似的牢房；转眼间他们一齐朝我们扑来——至少我感觉如此，因为那一刻我正把自己的灵魂交付给上帝，我笃定就在同一秒内，我将不复存活。

这一切恍如一场梦：我们没有听见人人料想中的、仇恨得逞的那种可怕咆哮，耳畔响起的是一个低沉而威严的耳语，它仿佛把我们从绝望的深渊里拖了出来，猛然带进了希望的光亮之中。

“只要你们信任我们，”那声音低低说道，“不要害怕，不出一个钟头，你们就会平平安安地出了巴黎。”

爸爸第一个明白过来出了什么事；即便在这恐怖时日最黑暗的时刻，他也从未失过方寸，此刻他十分平静而沉稳地问道：

===Original===
“What must we do?”

“Persuade the little ones not to be afraid, not to cry, to be as still and silent as may be,” continued the voice, which I felt must be that of one of God’s own angels, so exquisitely kind did it sound to my ear.

“They will be quiet and still without persuasion,” said papa; “eh, children?”

And Jean, André, and Marguerite murmured: “Yes!” whilst maman and I drew them closer to us and said everything we could think of to make them still more brave.

===Chinese===
“我们要做什么？”

“劝住小家伙们，别害怕，别哭，尽量安安静静、一动不动，”那声音继续说。它在我耳中听来如此温良仁慈，我觉得那必定是上帝座前某位天使的声音。

“不用劝，他们自会安安静静、一动不动的，”爸爸说，“对不对，孩子们？”

让、安德烈和玛格丽特低声应道：“对！”妈妈和我把他们搂得更紧，想得出的话都说尽了，只为让他们更加勇敢。

===Original===
And the whispering, commanding voice went on after awhile:

“Now will you allow yourselves to be muffled and bound, and, after that, will you swear that whatever happens, whatever you may see or hear, you will neither move nor speak? Not only your own lives, but those of many brave men will depend upon your fulfilment of this oath.”

Papa made no reply save to raise his hand and eyes up to where God surely was watching over us all. Maman said in her gentle, even voice:

“For myself and my children, I swear to do all that you tell us.”

===Chinese===
过了片刻，那低低的、威严的声音又说道：

“现在，你们可愿意让人蒙住头脸、捆住身子？过后，你们可愿意起誓：无论出了什么事，无论看见什么、听见什么，都绝不挪动、绝不开口？不只你们自己的性命，还有许多勇士的性命，都要看你们是否信守此誓。”

爸爸没有作答，只把手和眼睛抬向上天——上帝必定正在那里看护着我们众人。妈妈用她温柔平和的嗓音说道：

“我代表我自己和我的孩子们起誓：你们吩咐的一切，我们无不照办。”

===Original===
A great feeling of confidence had entered into her heart, just as it had done into mine. We looked at one another and knew that we were both thinking of the same thing: we were thinking of the brave Englishman and his gallant little band of heroes, about whom we had heard many wonderful tales﻿—how they had rescued a number of innocent people who were unjustly threatened with the guillotine; and we all knew that the tall figure, disguised in horrible rags, who spoke to us with such a gentle yet commanding voice, was the man whom rumour credited with supernatural powers, and who was known by the mysterious name of “The Scarlet Pimpernel.”

Hardly had we sworn to do his bidding than his friends most unceremoniously threw great pieces of sacking over our heads, and then proceeded to tie ropes round our bodies. At least, I know that that is what one of them was doing to me, and from one or two whispered words of command which reached my ear I concluded that papa and maman and the children were being dealt with in the same summary way.

I felt hot and stifled under that rough bit of sacking, but I would not have moved or even sighed for worlds. Strangely enough, as soon as my eyes and ears were shut off from the sounds and sights immediately round me, I once more became conscious of the horrible and awful din which was going on, not only on the other side of our prison walls, but inside the whole of the Abbaye building and in the street beyond.

Once more I heard those terrible howls of rage and of satisfied hatred, uttered by the assassins who were being paid by the government of our beautiful country to butcher helpless prisoners in their hundreds.

===Chinese===
一股巨大的信心涌进了她的心里，正如涌进了我的心里。我们对望一眼，便知道彼此想的是同一件事：我们想起了那位勇敢的英国人和他手下那一小队英豪——关于他们，我们听过许许多多神奇的传说，说他们救出过一批又一批无辜的人，那些人平白无故地要被送上断头台；我们全都知道，这个一身骇人破衣、用如此温柔而又威严的声音同我们说话的高大身影，正是人们传言身怀神通的那个人，正是以“红花侠”（the Scarlet Pimpernel）这个神秘名字闻名的那个人。

我们刚刚起誓听命于他，他的伙伴们便毫不客气地把大片大片的麻袋布罩到我们头上，接着又动手用绳子捆我们的身子。至少我知道，其中一个对我就是这么处置的；而凭着一两句飘进我耳朵的低声号令，我断定爸爸、妈妈和孩子们也正受着同样干脆的处置。

蒙在那块粗糙的麻袋布底下，我又热又闷，可说什么我也绝不肯动一动，连一口大气也不敢叹。说来奇怪，我的耳目一旦同身边的声响景象隔绝，便重新清醒地意识到那场骇人的喧嚣正闹得凶——它不仅在狱墙的另一侧，还响彻整座阿贝伊监狱，一直蔓延到墙外的大街。

我又一次听见了那阵阵狂怒与仇恨得逞的可怕嚎叫——发出嚎叫的是那些刺客，我们美丽国家的政府正出钱雇他们，成百成百地宰割手无寸铁的囚徒。

===Original===
Suddenly I felt myself hoisted up off my feet and slung up on to a pair of shoulders that must have been very powerful indeed, for I am no light weight, and once more I heard the voice, the very sound of which was delight, quite close to my ear this time, giving a brief and comprehensive command:

“All ready!﻿—remember your part﻿—en avant!”

Then it added in English. “Here, Tony, you start kicking against the door whilst we begin to shout!”

I loved those few words of English, and hoped that maman had heard them too, for it would confirm her﻿—as it did me﻿—in the happy knowledge that God and a brave man had taken our rescue in hand.

===Chinese===
忽然，我觉得自己被凌空掀起，甩上了一副肩膀——那肩膀必定极其有力，因为我绝不是个身子轻的人。我又一次听见了那个声音，这一回就在我耳边，单听那声响便叫人心喜；它下达了一道简短而周全的号令：

“都准备好了！——记住各自的任务——en avant！（前进！）”

接着它又用英语补了一句：“喂，托尼（Tony），你先动手砸门，我们这就开始喊叫！”

我真喜欢这几句英语，也盼着妈妈听见了，因为这会让她——正如让我一样——笃定地怀着那份欢喜的信念：我们的营救，已经由上帝和一个勇敢的人担当起来了。

===Original===
But from that moment we might have all been in the very antechamber of hell. I could hear the violent kicks against the heavy door of our prison, and our brave rescuers seemed suddenly to be transformed into a cageful of wild beasts. Their shouts and yells were as horrible as any that came to us from the outside, and I must say that the gentle, firm voice which I had learnt to love was as execrable as any I could hear.

Apparently the door would not yield, as the blows against it became more and more violent, and presently from somewhere above my head﻿—the window presumably﻿—there came a rough call, and a raucous laugh:

“Why? what in the name of —﻿—﻿— is happening here?”

And the voice near me answered back equally roughly: “A quarry of six﻿—but we are caught in this confounded trap﻿—get the door open for us, citizen﻿—we want to get rid of this booty and go in search for more.”

===Chinese===
可从那一刻起，我们简直像是全都置身在地狱的前厅里。我听得见朝我们牢房那扇沉重的大门猛踹的声音，我们勇敢的援救者们仿佛霎时间变成了一笼子野兽。他们的呼喝与嚎叫，同外面传来的任何声音一样骇人；我还得说一句，那个我已渐渐爱上的温柔而坚定的声音，骂出的话同我听见的任何粗话一样不堪。

门显然打不开，踹门声一阵凶过一阵；不多时，从我头顶上方的某处——想必就是那扇窗——传来一声粗野的喝问和一阵嘶哑的大笑：

“怎么搞的？这里到底在搞什么———名堂？”

我身旁那个声音同样粗野地回敬道：“猎获了六个——可我们叫这个该死的陷阱困住了——帮我们把门打开，公民——我们要把这批猎物脱手，好再去寻摸新的。”

===Original===
A horrible laugh was the reply from above, and the next instant I heard a terrific crash; the door had at last been burst open, either from within or without, I could not tell which, and suddenly all the din, the cries, the groans, the hideous laughter and bibulous songs which had sounded muffled up to now burst upon us with all their hideousness.

That was, I think, the most awful moment of that truly fearful hour. I could not have moved then, even had I wished or been able to do so; but I knew that between us all and a horrible, yelling, murdering mob there was now nothing﻿—except the hand of God and the heroism of a band of English gentlemen.

Together they gave a cry﻿—as loud, as terrifying as any that were uttered by the butchering crowd in the building, and with a wild rush they seemed to plunge with us right into the thick of the awful melee.

At least, that is what it all felt like to me, and afterwards I heard from our gallant rescuer himself that that is exactly what he and his friends did. There were eight of them altogether, and we four young ones had each been hoisted on a pair of devoted shoulders, whilst maman and papa were each carried by two men.

===Chinese===
上方回敬的是一阵狞笑；紧接着我听见一声轰然巨响，门终于被撞开了——从里还是从外撞开的，我分辨不出——霎时间，那一切方才听着发闷的喧嚣、呼叫、呻吟、狰狞的狂笑和醉醺醺的歌号，连同全部的狰狞，一齐向我们扑面而来。

我想，那是那个真正骇人的钟头里最恐怖的一刻。那时我便是有心挪动，也挪动不了；可我知道，此刻横在我们全家与一群狂呼嚎叫、杀人不眨眼的暴徒之间的，已经什么都不剩了——除了上帝的手，和一班英国绅士的英勇。

他们齐声发出一阵呐喊——同楼里那群屠夫发出的任何喊杀声一样响亮、一样骇人——随即一阵猛冲，仿佛驮着我们径直扎进了那场可怕混战的最中心。

至少，给我的感觉就是如此；后来我也从我们豪侠的援救者本人那里听说，他和他的朋友们干的确确实实就是这件事。他们一共八个人，我们四个孩子每人都被扛上了一副赤诚的肩膀，爸爸和妈妈则各由两个人抬着。

===Original===
I was lying across the finest pair of shoulders in the world, and close to me was beating the bravest heart on God’s earth.

Thus burdened, these eight noble English gentlemen charged right through an army of butchering, howling brutes, they themselves howling with the fiercest of them.

All around me I heard weird and terrific cries: “What ho! citizens﻿—what have you there?”

“Six aristos!” shouted my hero boldly as he rushed on, forging his way through the crowd.

===Chinese===
我横卧在世界上最好的一副肩膀上，紧贴着我的，是上帝的世间最勇敢的一颗心的跳动。

就这样身负重担，这八位高贵的英国绅士径直从一支嚎叫屠戮的畜生大军中冲杀而过，他们自己也同其中最凶悍的一路嚎叫着。

我听见四周响起种种怪诞而骇人的喝叫：“喂嗬！公民们——你们那儿扛的是什么？”

“六个贵族佬！”我的英雄一边往前冲、一边从人丛中劈开道路，朗声答道。

===Original===
“What are you doing with them?” yelled a raucous voice.

“Food for the starving fish in the river,” was the ready response. “Stand aside, citizen,” he added, with a round curse; “I have my orders from citizen Danton himself about these six aristos. You hinder me at your peril.”

He was challenged over and over again in the same way, and so were his friends who were carrying papa and maman and the children; but they were always ready with a reply, ready with an invective or a curse; with eyes that could not see, one could imagine them as hideous, as vengeful, as cruel as the rest of the crowd.

I think that soon I must have fainted from sheer excitement and terror, for I remember nothing more till I felt myself deposited on a hard floor, propped against the wall, and the stifling piece of sacking taken off my head and face.

===Chinese===
“拿他们去干什么？”一个嘶哑的声音吼道。

“扔进河里喂饿鱼的，”回答脱口而出。“让开，公民，”他补上一句，外加一串响亮的咒骂；“这六个贵族佬，是公民丹东（Danton）亲自给我的命令。你要碍我的事，后果自己担待。”

他一遍又一遍地受到同样的盘诘，扛着爸爸、妈妈和孩子们的伙伴们也一样；可他们总是对答如流，张口便是一句咒骂或恶言；在我这双被蒙住、什么也看不见的眼睛想象里，他们的模样必定同人群里其余的人一样狰狞、一样狠毒、一样残忍。

我想，不多时我便准是因极度的激奋与恐怖昏了过去，因为接下来我记得的，是自己被放到一处坚硬的地板上，靠墙倚着，那块闷人的麻袋布已经从我头上脸上摘掉了。

===Original===
I looked around me, dazed and bewildered; gradually the horrors of the past hour came back to me, and I had to close my eyes again, for I felt sick and giddy with the sheer memory of it all.

But presently I felt stronger and looked around me again. Jean and André were squatting in a corner close by, gazing wide-eyed at the group of men in filthy, ragged clothing, who sat round a deal table in the centre of a small, ill-furnished room.

Maman was lying on a horsehair sofa at the other end of the room, with Marguerite beside her, and papa sat in a low chair by her side, holding her hand.

The voice I loved was speaking in its quaint, somewhat drawly cadence:

===Chinese===
我神志恍惚地环顾四周；过去那个钟头的种种恐怖渐渐回到心头，我只得重新闭上眼睛，因为单是回想这一切，就叫我恶心眩晕。

但不多时我便觉得有了力气，又朝四下里望去。让和安德烈蹲在近旁的角落里，睁大眼睛望着那群衣衫肮脏褴褛的男人；他们围坐在一张松木桌旁，桌子摆在一间陈设简陋的小屋正中。

妈妈躺在屋子另一头的一张马鬃沙发上，玛格丽特依在她身边；爸爸坐在她身旁的低椅上，握着她的手。

我所爱的那个声音，正用它那古雅而略带拖腔的语调说着话：

===Original===
“You are quite safe now, my dear Monsieur Lemercier,” it said; “after Madame and the young people have had a rest, some of my friends will find you suitable disguises, and they will escort you out of Paris, as they have some really genuine passports in their possessions, which we obtain from time to time through the agency of a personage highly placed in this murdering government, and with the help of English banknotes. Those passports are not always unchallenged, I must confess,” added my hero with a quaint laugh; “but tonight everyone is busy murdering in one part of Paris, so the other parts are comparatively safe.”

Then he turned to one of his friends and spoke to him in English:

“You had better see this through, Tony,” he said, “with Hastings and Mackenzie. Three of you will be enough; I shall have need of the others.”

===Chinese===
“你们现在已经十分安全了，亲爱的勒梅西耶先生，”那声音说道，“等夫人和孩子们歇过一歇，我的几位伙伴会替你们置办合适的行头，护送你们出城——他们手头有几份货真价实的通行证，是我们不时通过这个杀人政府里一位身居高位的人物弄到手的，自然还少不了英格兰钞票的效力。我必须承认，那些通行证并非每次都畅行无阻，”我的英雄带着一丝古雅的笑补充道，“不过今夜人人都忙着在巴黎的这一区杀人，其余几处相比之下还算安全。”

随后他转向一位伙伴，用英语对他说：

“这件事你最好一办到底，托尼，”他说，“带上黑斯廷斯（Hastings）和麦肯齐（Mackenzie）。你们三个就够了；其余的人我还有用处。”

===Original===
No one seemed to question his orders. He had spoken, and the others made ready to obey. Just then papa spoke up:

“How are we going to thank you, sir?” he asked, speaking broken English, but with his habitual dignity of manner.

“By leaving your welfare in our hands, Monsieur,” replied our gallant rescuer quietly.

Papa tried to speak again, but the Englishman put up his hand to stop any further talk.

===Chinese===
似乎谁也不会去质疑他的号令。他既已发话，其余的人便整装听命。正在这时，爸爸开口了：

“我们该怎样报答您呢，先生？”他问，说的是结结巴巴的英语，仪态却仍是他惯有的那份雍容。

“把你们的安危托付到我们手里，先生，”我们豪侠的援救者平静地答道。

爸爸还想再说，可那英国人抬起手来，止住了他后面的话。

===Original===
“There is no time now, Monsieur,” he said with gentle courtesy. “I must leave you, as I have much work yet to do.”

“Where are you going, Blakeney?” asked one of the others.

“Back to the Abbaye prison,” he said; “there are other women and children to be rescued there!”

===Chinese===
“现在没有工夫了，先生，”他带着温和的谦彬说道，“我必须告辞，我还有许多事要办。”

“你要去哪儿，布莱克尼（Blakeney）？”其余人中有一个问道。

“回阿贝伊监狱，”他说，“那儿还有别的妇女儿童等着搭救呢！”

## Sir Percy Explains / ## 珀西爵士的解释

### I / ### 一

===Original===

It was not, Heaven help us all! a very uncommon occurrence these days: a woman almost unsexed by misery, starvation, and the abnormal excitement engendered by daily spectacles of revenge and of cruelty. They were to be met with every day, round every street corner, these harridans, more terrible far than were the men.
This one was still comparatively young, thirty at most; would have been good-looking too, for the features were really delicate, the nose chiselled, the brow straight, the chin round and small. But the mouth! Heavens, what a mouth! Hard and cruel and thin-lipped; and those eyes! sunken and rimmed with purple; eyes that told tales of sorrow and, yes! of degradation. The crowd stood round her, sullen and apathetic; poor, miserable wretches like herself, staring at her antics with lacklustre eyes and an ever-recurrent contemptuous shrug of the shoulders.
The woman was dancing, contorting her body in the small circle of light formed by a flickering lantern which was hung across the street from house to house, striking the muddy pavement with her shoeless feet, all to the sound of a be-ribboned tambourine which she struck now and again with her small, grimy hand. From time to time she paused, held out the tambourine at arm’s length, and went the round of the spectators, asking for alms. But at her approach the crowd at once seemed to disintegrate, to melt into the humid evening air; it was but rarely that a greasy token fell into the outstretched tambourine. Then as the woman started again to dance the crowd gradually reassembled, and stood, hands in pockets, lips still sullen and contemptuous, but eyes watchful of the spectacle. There were such few spectacles these days, other than the monotonous processions of tumbrils with their load of aristocrats for the guillotine!
So the crowd watched, and the woman danced. The lantern overhead threw a weird light on red caps and tricolour cockades, on the sullen faces of the men and the shoulders of the women, on the dancer’s weird antics and her flying, tattered skirts. She was obviously tired, as a poor, performing cur might be, or a bear prodded along to uncongenial buffoonery. Every time that she paused and solicited alms with her tambourine the crowd dispersed, and some of them laughed because she insisted.

===Chinese===

上天可怜我们大家吧！这样的景象在这年头已算不得稀罕：一个女人，被苦难、饥饿，加上日日目睹的复仇与残忍场面激起的反常亢奋，磨蚀得几乎不复有女人的模样。这样的悍妇，天天都能碰上，街角拐弯处就有，远比男人可怕得多。
这一个还比较年轻，至多三十岁；本来也会是个好看的女人，因为五官确实生得清秀，鼻子雕琢般挺秀，额头端正，下巴圆而小巧。可是那张嘴！老天爷，那是怎样一张嘴啊！又硬又狠，嘴唇极薄；还有那双眼睛！深陷着，眼圈发紫；那是一双诉说着悲哀——是的！也诉说着堕落的眼睛。人群围着她，阴沉而麻木；都是和她一样的穷苦可怜虫，用黯淡无光的眼睛呆望着她的疯态，肩膀一耸又一耸，满是不屑。
那女人在跳舞，在一盏从街这头的房子拉到那头房子、摇曳不定的灯笼投下的一小圈光晕里扭动着身躯，赤着的双脚拍打着泥泞的路面，一切和着一面缀着缎带的铃鼓——她时不时用瘦小污黑的手击它几下。她不时停下来，把铃鼓伸直了递出去，绕着看客讨钱。可她一走近，人群便仿佛顿时散开，消融在湿漉漉的夜气里；难得有一枚油腻的铜子儿落进摊开的铃鼓。等女人重新跳起来，人群又渐渐聚拢，站在那儿，双手插兜，嘴上依旧挂着阴沉的轻蔑，眼睛却盯着这场把戏。这年头这样的把戏太少了，除了那一辆辆载满贵族佬押赴断头台的死囚车单调地驶过！
于是人群看着，女人跳着。头顶的灯笼把怪异的光投在红帽子和三色帽徽上，投在男人们阴沉的脸和女人们的肩膀上，投在跳舞女人怪诞的扭摆和飘飞的破裙上。她显然累了，像一条卖艺的可怜癞皮狗，又像一头被驱赶着扮演不情愿把戏的熊。每回她停下来摇着铃鼓讨钱，人群便散去，有几个还因为她不依不饶而哄笑。

===Original===

“*Voyons*,” she said with a weird attempt at gaiety, “a couple of sous for the entertainment, citizen! You have stood here half an hour. You can’t have it all for nothing, what?”
The man﻿—young, square-shouldered, thick-lipped, with the look of a bully about his well-clad person﻿—retorted with a coarse insult, which the woman resented. There were high words; the crowd for the most part ranged itself on the side of the bully. The woman backed against the wall nearest to her, held feeble, emaciated hands up to her ears in a vain endeavour to shut out the hideous jeers and ribald jokes which were the natural weapons of this untamed crowd.
Soon blows began to rain; not a few fell upon the unfortunate woman. She screamed, and the more she screamed the louder did the crowd jeer, the uglier became its temper. Then suddenly it was all over. How it happened the woman could not tell. She had closed her eyes, feeling sick and dizzy; but she had heard a loud call, words spoken in English (a language which she understood), a pleasant laugh, and a brief but violent scuffle. After that the hurrying retreat of many feet, the click of sabots on the uneven pavement and patter of shoeless feet, and then silence.

===Chinese===

“Voyons（喂），”她说道，硬装出几分古怪的欢快，“看了半天戏，总得赏两个苏（sou）吧，公民！您在这儿站了半个钟头了。总不能白看一场，是吧？”
那男人——年纪轻轻，肩膀方阔，嘴唇厚厚的，衣冠齐整的一身透着流氓气——回了一句粗鄙的辱骂，女人当即回敬。两人高声争吵起来；人群大半站到了那恶棍一边。女人退到离她最近的墙根，把瘦弱干枯的双手举到耳边，想挡住那难听的奚落和下流的哄笑——那原是这群未驯化的暴民惯用的武器，却只是徒劳。
很快拳脚如雨点般落下；不少都落在了那不幸的女人身上。她尖叫起来，她越叫，人群哄笑得越响，火气也越旺。随后一切突然结束了。怎么回事，女人自己也说不清。她闭上了眼睛，只觉得恶心眩晕；但她听见一声响亮的呼喝，几句英语——一种她听得懂的语言——一阵爽朗的笑，和一场短促而激烈的扭打。此后便是许多人仓皇奔逃的脚步声，木屐磕在坎坷路面上的咔咔声和赤脚的啪嗒声，然后，一片寂静。

===Original===

She had fallen on her knees and was cowering against the wall, had lost consciousness probably for a minute or two. Then she heard that pleasant laugh again and the soft drawl of the English tongue.
“I love to see those beggars scuttling off, like so many rats to their burrows, don’t you, Ffoulkes?”
“They didn’t put up much fight, the cowards!” came from another voice, also in English. “A dozen of them against this wretched woman. What had best be done with her?”
“I’ll see to her,” rejoined the first speaker. “You and Tony had best find the others. Tell them I shall be round directly.”
It all seemed like a dream. The woman dared not open her eyes lest reality﻿—hideous and brutal﻿—once more confronted her. Then all at once she felt that her poor, weak body, encircled by strong arms, was lifted off the ground, and that she was being carried down the street, away from the light projected by the lantern overhead, into the sheltering darkness of a yawning porte-cochère. But she was not then fully conscious.

===Chinese===

她跪倒在地，缩在墙根，大约有一两分钟失去了知觉。然后她又听见了那爽朗的笑声，还有英语那种柔和拖长的腔调。
“我就爱看这帮家伙抱头鼠窜，活像一群逃回洞去的耗子，你说是不是，福克斯（Ffoulkes）？”
“他们没怎么还手，这帮胆小鬼！”另一个声音说道，讲的也是英语。“十几个大男人，欺负这么个可怜的女人。拿她怎么办才好？”
“我来照应她，”先说话的那个人应道。“你和托尼最好去找其他人。告诉他们我随后就到。”
这一切恍如梦境。女人不敢睁开眼睛，生怕那丑恶而残忍的现实再次扑到面前。接着她忽然觉得，自己那可怜虚弱的身体被一双有力的臂膀拢住，抱离了地面，顺着街道被抱走了，离开头顶灯笼投下的光，进了黑洞洞的车马大门（porte-cochère）掩身的黑暗里。不过那时她神志并不清醒。

### II / ### 二

===Original===

When she reopened her eyes she was in what appeared to be the lodge of a concierge. She was lying on a horsehair sofa. There was a sense of warmth and of security around her. No wonder that it still seemed like a dream. Before her stood a man, tall and straight, surely a being from another world﻿—or so he appeared to the poor wretch who, since uncountable time, had set eyes on none but the most miserable dregs of struggling humanity, who had seen little else but rags, and faces either cruel or wretched. This man was clad in a huge caped coat, which made his powerful figure seem preternaturally large. His hair was fair and slightly curly above his low, square brow; the eyes beneath their heavy lids looked down on her with unmistakable kindness.
The poor woman struggled to her feet. With a quick and pathetically humble gesture she drew her ragged, muddy skirts over her ankles and her tattered kerchief across her breast.
“I had best go now, Monsieur﻿ ﻿… citizen,” she murmured, while a hot flush rose to the roots of her unkempt hair. “I must not stop here.﻿ ﻿… I﻿—”
“You are not going, Madame,” he broke in, speaking now in perfect French and with a great air of authority, as one who is accustomed to being implicitly obeyed, “until you have told me how, a lady of culture and of refinement, comes to be masquerading as a street-dancer. The game is a dangerous one, as you have experienced tonight.”

===Chinese===

等她再睁开眼睛，她已置身在一间像是门房小屋的屋子里，躺在一张马鬃沙发上。四周有一种温暖而安全的感觉。难怪她仍觉得像在梦中。她面前站着一个男人，高大挺拔，想必是来自另一个世界的人物——至少在这个可怜虫眼里是如此，因为不知已有多久，她见过的只剩挣扎人间最悲惨的渣滓，见惯的只有破衣烂衫，和不是凶横便是愁苦的面孔。这人裹着一件宽大的披肩式大衣，衬得他魁伟的身形异乎寻常地庞大。低平方正的前额上覆着淡色的微鬈头发；厚眼睑下的那双眼睛俯视着她，流露着不容错认的慈和。
可怜的女人挣扎着站起身来。她慌忙做了一个卑微得令人心酸的姿势，把破烂沾泥的裙子拉下来遮住脚踝，又把碎成布片的头巾拢上胸口。
“我现在最好走吧，Monsieur（先生）……公民，”她喃喃地说，一股热辣辣的红晕直冒到蓬乱头发的发根。“我不该在这儿停留……我——”
“您先别走，夫人，”他打断她，此刻说的是一口地道的法语，带着十足的威严气派，像是个说一不二惯了的人，“您得先告诉我，一位有教养、有身份的贵妇人，怎么会扮成个街头舞女。这把戏危险得很，您今晚已经领教过了。”

===Original===

“It is no game, Monsieur﻿ ﻿… citizen,” she stammered; “nor yet a masquerade. I have been a street-dancer all my life, and﻿—”
By way of an answer he took her hand, always with that air of authority which she never thought to resent.
“This is not a street-dancer’s hand; Madame,” he said quietly. “Nor is your speech that of the people.”
She drew her hand away quickly, and the flush on her haggard face deepened.
“If you will honour me with your confidence, Madame,” he insisted.

===Chinese===

“这不是什么把戏，Monsieur……公民，”她结结巴巴地说；“也不是什么化装。我一辈子就是个街头舞女，而且——”
他没有答话，只拿起她的手，依旧带着那股威严之气——而她竟从没想过去恼他。
“这不是一双街头舞女的手，夫人，”他轻声说。“您的谈吐也不是平民百姓的谈吐。”
她赶紧把手抽了回来，憔悴脸上的红晕更深了。
“如果您肯赏光对我说实话，夫人，”他坚持道。

===Original===

The kindly words, the courtesy of the man, went to the poor creature’s heart. She fell back upon the sofa and with her face buried in her arms she sobbed out her heart for a minute or two. The man waited quite patiently. He had seen many women weep these days, and had dried many a tear through deeds of valour and of self-sacrifice, which were forever recorded in the hearts of those whom he had succoured.
When this poor woman had succeeded in recovering some semblance of self-control, she turned her wan, tear-stained face to him and said simply:
“My name is Madeleine Lannoy, Monsieur. My husband was killed during the émeutes at Versailles, whilst defending the persons of the Queen and of the royal children against the fury of the mob. When I was a girl I had the misfortune to attract the attentions of a young doctor named Jean Paul Marat. You have heard of him, Monsieur?”
The other nodded.

===Chinese===

这温存的话语，这人的彬彬有礼，直渗进这可怜人的心里。她跌坐在沙发上，把脸埋进臂弯里，痛痛快快哭了一两分钟。那男人极有耐心地等着。这些日子他见过多少女人哭泣，也用英勇和自我牺牲的壮举拭干过多少眼泪——那些壮举，永远铭刻在他所搭救的人们心里。
等这可怜的女人终于恢复了一点自持的样子，她把苍白泪污的脸转向他，简单地说：
“我叫玛德莱娜·拉努瓦（Madeleine Lannoy），先生。我丈夫死在凡尔赛的暴乱里，当时他为了保卫王后和王家孩子们，抵挡暴民的狂怒。我做姑娘的时候，不幸引来一位年轻医生的注目，他名叫让-保罗·马拉（Jean Paul Marat）。您听说过他吧，先生？”
对方点了点头。

===Original===

“You know him, perhaps,” she continued, “for what he is: the most cruel and revengeful of men. A few years ago he threw up his lucrative appointment as Court physician to Monseigneur le Comte d’Artois, and gave up the profession of medicine for that of journalist and politician. Politician! Heaven help him! He belongs to the most bloodthirsty section of revolutionary brigands. His creed is pillage, murder, and revenge; and he chooses to declare that it is I who, by rejecting his love, drove him to these foul extremities. May God forgive him that abominable lie! The evil we do, Monsieur, is within us; it does not come from circumstance. I, in the meanwhile, was a happy wife. My husband, M. de Lannoy, who was an officer in the army, idolised me. We had one child, a boy﻿—”
She paused, with another catch in her throat. Then she resumed, with calmness that, in view of the tale she told, sounded strangely weird:
“In June last year my child was stolen from me﻿—stolen by Marat in hideous revenge for the supposed wrong which I had done him. The details of that execrable outrage are of no importance. I was decoyed from home one day through the agency of a forged message purporting to come from a very dear friend whom I knew to be in grave trouble at the time. Oh! the whole thing was thoroughly well thought out, I can assure you!” she continued, with a harsh laugh which ended in a heartrending sob. “The forged message, the suborned servant, the threats of terrible reprisals if anyone in the village gave me the slightest warning or clue. When the whole miserable business was accomplished, I was just like a trapped animal inside a cage, held captive by immovable bars of obstinate silence and cruel indifference. No one would help me. No one ostensibly knew anything; no one had seen anything, heard anything. The child was gone! My servants, the people in the village﻿—some of whom I could have sworn were true and sympathetic﻿—only shrugged their shoulders. ‘*Que voulez-vous, Madame*? Children of bourgeois as well as of aristos were often taken up by the State to be brought up as true patriots and no longer pampered like so many lapdogs.’
“Three days later I received a letter from that inhuman monster, Jean Paul Marat. He told me that he had taken my child away from me, not from any idea of revenge for my disdain in the past, but from a spirit of pure patriotism. My boy, he said, should not be brought up with the same ideas of bourgeois effeteness and love of luxury which had disgraced the nation for centuries. No! he should be reared amongst men who had realised the true value of fraternity and equality and the ideal of complete liberty for the individual to lead his own life, unfettered by senseless prejudices of education and refinement. Which means, Monsieur,” the poor woman went on with passionate misery, “that my child is to be reared up in the company of all that is most vile and most degraded in the disease-haunted slums of indigent Paris; that, with the connivance of that execrable fiend Marat, my only son will, mayhap, come back to me one day a potential thief, a criminal probably, a drink-sodden reprobate at best. Such things are done every day in this glorious Revolution of ours﻿—done in the sacred name of France and of Liberty. And the moral murder of my child is to be my punishment for daring to turn a deaf ear to the indign passion of a brute!”

===Chinese===

“您也许知道他如今是个什么样的人，”她接着说，“最残忍、最睚眦必报的男人。几年前他辞掉了阿图瓦伯爵（Monseigneur le Comte d'Artois）府上御医的肥差，抛开医学这一行，改行当了新闻记者和政治家。政治家！上天保佑他吧！他属于革命暴徒中最嗜血的那一伙。他的信条就是劫掠、杀戮和复仇；他还偏要宣称，是我拒绝了他的爱情，才把他逼到这些卑污的绝路上去。愿上帝宽恕他那个可恶的谎言！先生，我们作的恶，根源在我们自己心里，并不是环境使然。而我呢，那时是个幸福的妻子。我丈夫拉努瓦先生是军队里的军官，把我当成心肝宝贝。我们有一个孩子，是个男孩——”
她停住了，喉咙又哽了一下。然后她继续说下去，那种平静的口气，想想她所讲的事，听来古怪得吓人：
“去年六月，我的孩子被人从我身边偷走了——是马拉偷的，作为对我莫须有的亏负他的可怖报复。那桩可憎罪行的细节无关紧要。有一天，一封伪造的信把我骗出了家门，信上冒充一位至交好友的名义——我知道她当时正陷在极大的麻烦里。哦！这整件事筹划得滴水不漏，我可以向您担保！”她接着说，发出一声刺耳的笑，笑到末了化作令人心碎的呜咽。“伪造的信件，被收买的仆人，还有恐吓——谁敢在村子里向我透半点口风、给半点线索，就有可怕的报复等着谁。等这桩可鄙的勾当全部办妥，我就像一头关在笼中的困兽，被执拗的沉默和残忍的冷漠这两根纹丝不动的铁栏困住了。没有人肯帮我。明面上谁都不知道，谁都没看见什么，没听见什么。孩子没了！我的仆人们，村里的人们——有几个我本可以发誓是忠心而同情的——只是耸耸肩膀。‘Que voulez-vous, Madame（有什么法子呢，夫人）？资产阶级的孩子也好，贵族佬的孩子也好，常被国家收走，去培养成真正的爱国者，再也不像哈巴狗似的娇生惯养。’”
“三天后，我收到了那个没有心肝的怪物让-保罗·马拉的一封信。他告诉我，他带走我的孩子，绝不是为了报复我往日的轻慢，而是出于一片纯粹的爱国心。他说，我的儿子不可以在资产阶级的萎靡和对奢侈的贪恋中长大——正是那些观念害得这个民族蒙羞了几个世纪。不！他要在这样的人中间长大：他们早已领悟博爱和平等的真正价值，信仰每个人都有完全的自由去过自己的生活，不受教养和文雅这些无聊偏见的束缚。这就是说，先生，”可怜的女人带着痛切的悲苦继续说，“我的孩子要在赤贫巴黎那疾病滋生的贫民窟里，与最卑鄙最堕落的货色为伴地长大；在那个可憎的恶魔马拉的纵容下，我唯一的儿子，也许有一天回到我跟前时，已经是个准小偷，多半是个罪犯，至多也不过是个泡在酒里的败类。这样的事，在我们这场光荣的革命里天天都在做——以法兰西和自由的神圣名义做着。而我孩子的灵魂谋杀，就是我胆敢对一头畜生的狂暴情欲置之不理的惩罚！”

===Original===

Once more she paused, and when the melancholy echo of her broken voice had died away in the narrow room, not another murmur broke the stillness of this faraway corner of the great city.
The man did not move. He stood looking down upon the poor woman before him, a world of pity expressed in his deep-set eyes. Through the absolute silence around there came the sound as of a gentle flutter, the current of cold air, mayhap, sighing through the ill-fitting shutters, or the soft, weird soughing made by unseen things. The man’s heart was full of pity, and it seemed as if the Angel of Compassion had come at his bidding and enfolded the sorrowing woman with his wings.
A moment or two later she was able to finish her pathetic narrative.

===Chinese===

她又一次停了下来。当她那断断续续的语声的凄凉回响在这间狭屋里消散之后，大城里这个偏远角落的寂静，再没有一点声息来打破。
那男人一动不动。他站在那里，俯视着面前这可怜的女人，深陷的双眼里盛满无限的怜悯。四周绝对的寂静里传来一阵轻轻的扑翼似的声响，也许是冷风穿过关不严的百叶窗时的叹息，也许是看不见的东西发出的柔和而怪异的沙沙声。这男人的心里充满了怜悯，仿佛怜悯天使应他的召唤而来，张开翅膀裹住了这个悲伤的女人。
过了一两分钟，她才得以讲完她那悲惨的身世。

===Original===

“Do you marvel, Monsieur,” she said, “that I am still sane﻿—still alive? But I only live to find my child. I try and keep my reason in order to fight the devilish cunning of a brute on his own ground. Up to now all my inquiries have been in vain. At first I squandered money, tried judicial means, set an army of sleuthhounds on the track. I tried bribery, corruption. I went to the wretch himself and abased myself in the dust before him. He only laughed at me and told me that his love for me had died long ago; he now was lavishing its treasures upon the faithful friend and companion﻿—that awful woman, Simonne Evrard﻿—who had stood by him in the darkest hours of his misfortunes. Then it was that I decided to adopt different tactics. Since my child was to be reared in the midst of murderers and thieves, I, too, would haunt their abodes. I became a street-singer, dancer, what you will. I wear rags now and solicit alms. I haunt the most disreputable cabarets in the lowest slums of Paris. I listen and I spy; I question every man, woman, and child who might afford some clue, give me some indication. There is hardly a house in these parts that I have not visited and whence I have not been kicked out as an importunate beggar or worse. Gradually I am narrowing the circle of my investigations. Presently I shall get a clue. I shall! I know I shall! God cannot allow this monstrous thing to go on!”
Again there was silence. The poor woman had completely broken down. Shame, humiliation, passionate grief, had made of her a mere miserable wreckage of humanity.
The man waited awhile until she was composed, then he said simply:

===Chinese===

“您觉得奇怪吗，先生，”她说，“我居然还神志清醒——居然还活着？可我活着，只是为了找回我的孩子。我竭力保住自己的理智，好用那畜生自己的伎俩去对付他那魔鬼般的狡诈。直到现在，我一切打听都归徒劳。起初我大把花钱，走诉讼的门路，放出一大群暗探去追线索。我试过贿赂，试过收买。我找到那恶棍本人，在他面前卑屈到了尘埃里。他只是嘲笑我，说他对我的爱情早已死掉；如今他正把那份情的宝藏，大把挥霍在那个忠实的女友兼伴侣身上——那个可怕的女人，西莫娜·埃夫拉尔（Simonne Evrard）——她在他最倒霉的黑暗日子里守着他。就是在那时，我决定改换法子。既然我的孩子要在杀人犯和窃贼中间长大，那我也去他们的巢穴里出没。我做起了街头卖唱的、跳舞的，什么都做。如今我穿着破衣讨钱。巴黎最下等贫民窟里最声名狼藉的酒馆，我一处一处都泡。我听，我窥探；凡可能提供一点线索、给我一点风声的男人、女人、孩子，我个个都打听。这一带几乎没有什么人家我没进过，也没哪家没把我当纠缠不休的乞丐——或者更糟——给踢出来过。我渐渐缩小侦察的圈子。很快我就会摸到线索的。一定！我知道我一定会的！上帝不能容许这样骇人听闻的事继续下去！”
又是一阵沉默。可怜的女人彻底垮了。羞耻、屈辱、灼热的悲痛，把她磨成了人间残破的废墟。
那男人等了一会儿，等她平静下来，才简单地说：

===Original===

“You have suffered terribly, Madame; but chiefly, I think, because you have been alone in your grief. You have brooded over it until it has threatened your reason. Now, if you will allow me to act as your friend, I will pledge you my word that I will find your son for you. Will you trust me sufficiently to give up your present methods and place yourself entirely in my hands? There are more than a dozen gallant gentlemen, who are my friends, and who will help me in my search. But for this I must have a free hand, and only help from you when I require it. I can find you lodgings where you will be quite safe under the protection of my wife, who is as like an angel as any man or woman I have ever met on this earth. When your son is once more in your arms, you will, I hope, accompany us to England, where so many of your friends have already found a refuge. If this meets with your approval, Madame, you may command me, for with your permission I mean to be your most devoted servant.”
Dante, in his wild imaginations of hell and of purgatory and fleeting glimpses of paradise, never put before us the picture of a soul that was lost and found heaven, after a cycle of despair. Nor could Madeleine Lannoy ever explain her feelings at that moment, even to herself. To begin with, she could not quite grasp the reality of this ray of hope, which came to her at the darkest hour of her misery. She stared at the man before her as she would on an ethereal vision; she fell on her knees and buried her face in her hands.
What happened afterwards she hardly knew; she was in a state of semiconsciousness. When she once more woke to reality, she was in comfortable lodgings; she moved and talked and ate and lived like a human being. She was no longer a pariah, an outcast, a poor, half-demented creature, insentient save for an infinite capacity for suffering. She suffered still, but she no longer despaired. There had been such marvellous power and confidence in that man’s voice when he said: “I pledge you my word.” Madeleine Lannoy lived now in hope and a sweet sense of perfect mental and bodily security. Around her there was an influence, too, a presence which she did not often see, but always felt to be there: a woman, tall and graceful and sympathetic, who was always ready to cheer, to comfort, and to help. Her name was Marguerite. Madame Lannoy never knew her by any other. The man had spoken of her as being as like an angel as could be met on this earth, and poor Madeleine Lannoy fully agreed with him.

===Chinese===

“您受了大苦了，夫人；但我想，最要紧的还是您一直独自在苦水里泡着。您把这事在心里翻来覆去，眼看就要伤了神志。眼下，如果您肯让我以朋友自任，我向您担保，我一定替您找回您的儿子。您能不能信得过我，放下您现在的法子，把自己全然交到我手里？我有十多位豪侠的绅士朋友，他们都愿意帮我寻访。不过办这件事，我必须放手去干，只在需要时才请您相助。我可以给您安排一处住所，有我妻子照应，保管平安无事——在我生平见过的人里，无论男女，再没有比她更像天使的了。等您的儿子重新回到您怀里，我希望您能随我们去英国，您的许多朋友早已在那里觅得安身之所了。夫人，您若允准，尽管吩咐我；蒙您应允，我愿做您最忠心的仆人。”
但丁凭着他那地狱与炼狱的狂想和对天堂的惊鸿一瞥，也从不曾给我们描画过这样一个灵魂：历尽绝望的轮回之后，迷失了，却又找到了天堂。玛德莱娜·拉努瓦自己也永远说不清那一刻的心情，连对她自己都说不清。首先，她简直抓不住这道希望的微光的现实——它偏偏在她苦难最黑暗的时刻降临。她怔怔地望着面前的男人，如同望一个缥缈的幻象；随即她双膝跪倒，把脸埋进了双手。
后来发生了什么，她几乎不知道；她一直处于半昏迷的状态。等她再度回到现实中，她已经住在一处舒适的寓所里；她行动、说话、吃饭、生活，都像个人了。她不再是贱民，不再是被抛弃的人，不再是那种除了无尽的受苦能力之外什么都不剩的半疯的可怜虫。她仍然痛苦，但不再绝望。那男人说“我向您担保”的时候，声音里有一种惊人的力量和自信。玛德莱娜·拉努瓦如今活在希望里，活在身心完全无忧的甜美感觉里。她周围还有一种影响，一个身影——不常见到，却总觉得她在那儿：一个女人，高挑、优雅、富于同情心，随时准备鼓舞人、安慰人、帮助人。她的名字叫玛格丽特。拉努瓦夫人从来只知道她叫这个名字。那男人说起她时，说她是人世间所能遇见的最像天使的人，可怜的玛德莱娜·拉努瓦对此完全赞同。

### III / ### 三

===Original===

Even that bloodthirsty tiger, Jean Paul Marat, has had his apologists. His friends have called him a martyr, a selfless and incorruptible exponent of social and political ideals. We may take it that Simonne Evrard loved him, for a more impassioned obituary speech was, mayhap, never spoken than the one which she delivered before the National Assembly in honour of that sinister demagogue, whose writings and activities will forever sully some of the really fine pages of that revolutionary era.
But with those apologists we have naught to do. History has talked its fill of the inhuman monster. With the more intimate biographists alone has this true chronicle any concern. It is one of these who tells us that on or about the eighteenth day of Messidor, in the year I of the Republic (a date which corresponds with the sixth of July, 1793, of our own calendar), Jean Paul Marat took an additional man into his service, at the instance of Jeannette Maréchal, his cook and maid-of-all-work. Marat was at this time a martyr to an unpleasant form of skin disease, brought on by the terrible privations which he had endured during the few years preceding his association with Simonne Evrard, the faithful friend and housekeeper, whose small fortune subsequently provided him with some degree of comfort.
The man whom Jeannette Maréchal, the cook, introduced into the household of No. 30, Rue des Cordeliers, that worthy woman had literally picked one day out of the gutter where he was grabbing for scraps of food like some wretched starving cur. He appeared to be known to the police of the section, his identity book proclaiming him to be one Paul Molé, who had served his time in gaol for larceny. He professed himself willing to do any work required of him, for the merest pittance and some kind of roof over his head. Simonne Evrard allowed Jeannette to take him in, partly out of compassion and partly with a view to easing the woman’s own burden, the only other domestic in the house﻿—a man named Bas﻿—being more interested in politics and the meetings of the Club des Jacobins than he was in his master’s ailments. The man Molé, moreover, appeared to know something of medicine and of herbs and how to prepare the warm baths which alone eased the unfortunate Marat from pain. He was powerfully built, too, and though he muttered and grumbled a great deal, and indulged in prolonged fits of sulkiness, when he would not open his mouth to anyone, he was, on the whole, helpful and good-tempered.

===Chinese===

就连让-保罗·马拉这头嗜血的猛虎，也有过为他辩白的人。他的朋友们称他为殉道者，说他无私、不受腐蚀，是社会政治理想的宣扬者。我们不妨相信西莫娜·埃夫拉尔是爱他的，因为很难说还有什么悼词，比她在国民议会（National Assembly）面前为那个阴险的煽动家所致的悼词更充满激情了——此人的文字和行径，将永远玷污那个革命时代真正光辉的篇章。
不过那些辩护士与我们无关。历史对这个丧尽天良的怪物已经说得够多了。这部真实的纪事，只与较为私密的传记作者们打交道。其中一位告诉我们：共和元年穗月（Messidor）十八日前后——按我们自己的历法，即 1793 年 7 月 6 日——让-保罗·马拉经他的厨娘兼打杂女仆让内特·马雷夏尔（Jeannette Maréchal）的说项，新收了一个仆人。马拉此时正被一种讨厌的皮肤病折磨着，那是他结识忠实的女友兼管家西莫娜·埃夫拉尔之前几年里经受可怕的穷困落下的，而西莫娜的那一小笔家私后来才给了他几分安逸。
厨娘让内特·马雷夏尔带进科德利埃街（Rue des Cordeliers）30 号那户人家的这个男人，说实在的，是她有一天从阴沟里捡来的——当时他正像一条饿得半死的癞皮狗，在沟里刨抢残羹剩饭。本区的警察似乎认得他，他的身份证上写着：保罗·莫莱（Paul Molé），因盗窃坐过牢。他自己声称，什么活计都愿意干，只求一口聊以糊口的饭食和一个遮头的屋顶。西莫娜·埃夫拉尔允许让内特收留他，一半出于怜悯，一半也想减轻那女人的负担——家里原有另一个仆人，一个名叫巴斯（Bas）的男人，一心只热衷政治和雅各宾俱乐部（Club des Jacobins）的集会，对主人的病痛倒不甚上心。再者，这莫莱似乎还懂得些医道药草，会调制热药浴——唯有药浴能减轻不幸的马拉的痛楚。他身板也结实；虽然嘴里嘟嘟囔囔，抱怨个没完，还动不动闷上半天，谁也不理，但总的说来，还算勤快和气。

===Original===

There must also have been something about his whole wretched personality which made a strong appeal to the “Friend of the People,” for it is quite evident that within a few days Paul Molé had won no small measure of his master’s confidence.
Marat, sick, fretful, and worried, had taken an unreasoning dislike to his servant Bas. He was thankful to have a stranger about him, a man who was as miserable as he himself had been a very little while ago; who, like himself, had lived in cellars and in underground burrows, and lived on the scraps of food which even street-curs had disdained.
On the seventh day following Molé’s entry into the household, and while the latter was preparing his employer’s bath, Marat said abruptly to him:
“You’ll go as far as the Chemin de Pantin today for me, citizen. You know your way?”
“I can find it, what?” muttered Molé, who appeared to be in one of his surly moods.
“You will have to go very circumspectly,” Marat went on, in his cracked and feeble voice. “And see to it that no one spies upon your movements. I have many enemies, citizen﻿ ﻿… one especially﻿ ﻿… a woman.﻿ ﻿… She is always prying and spying on me.﻿ ﻿… So beware of any woman you see lurking about at your heels.”

===Chinese===

他那副整个卑微可怜的做派里，想必也有什么地方正中“人民之友”的下怀，因为很明显，不出几天，保罗·莫莱便赢得了他的主人相当程度的信任。
马拉多病、烦躁、忧心，对仆人巴斯生出一种毫无来由的厌恶。身边有个陌生人伺候，他倒觉庆幸——这人不久前还和自己一样落魄；同自己一样住过地窖、钻过地洞，靠连街上的野狗都嫌弃的残羹活命。
莫莱进这家门的第七天，他正伺候主人洗浴，马拉突然对他说：
“你今天替我往潘坦路（Chemin de Pantin）去一趟，公民。认得路吗？”
“找得到，是吧？”莫莱咕哝道，像是又犯了闷性子。
“你路上得万分小心，”马拉用他那沙哑虚弱的声音接着说。“留神别叫人盯上你的行踪。我的仇家多得很，公民……有一个尤其是……是个女人……她总在窥探我、侦察我……所以，但凡见有女人在你身后鬼鬼祟祟，务必当心。”

===Original===

Molé gave a half-audible grunt in reply.
“You had best go after dark,” the other rejoined after awhile. “Come back to me after nine o’clock. It is not far to the Chemin de Pantin﻿—just where it intersects the Route de Meaux. You can get there and back before midnight. The people will admit you. I will give you a ring﻿—the only thing I possess.﻿ ﻿… It has little or no value,” he added with a harsh, grating laugh. “It will not be worth your while to steal it. You will have to see a brat and report to me on his condition﻿—his appearance, what?﻿ ﻿… Talk to him a bit.﻿ ﻿… See what he says and let me know. It is not difficult.”
“No, citizen.”
Molé helped the suffering wretch into his bath. Not a movement, not a quiver of the eyelid betrayed one single emotion which he may have felt﻿—neither loathing nor sympathy, only placid indifference. He was just a half-starved menial, thankful to accomplish any task for the sake of satisfying a craving stomach. Marat stretched out his shrunken limbs in the herbal water with a sigh of well-being.

===Chinese===

莫莱半听不听地哼了一声算是回答。
“你最好天黑以后再去，”对方过了一会儿又接着说。“九点钟以后回来见我。潘坦路不远——就在它与莫城大道（Route de Meaux）交口的地方。你半夜前赶得个来回。那家人会放你进去。我给你一枚戒指——那是我仅有的一样东西……它几乎一文不值，”他又添了一句，笑得刺耳难听。“犯不着为它动偷心。你要去看一个小崽子，回来向我禀报他的情形——他的模样，嗯？……跟他聊几句……看他都说些什么，回来告诉我。这不难。”
“不难，公民。”
莫莱搀着那个受罪的人进了浴盆。他纵有什么心思，动作上、眼皮上都不露一丝痕迹——既无憎恶，也无同情，只有平静的漠然。他不过是个饿得半死的下人，只要能填饱肚子，干什么活都感激。马拉把干瘦的四肢伸进药草水里，舒坦地叹了口气。

===Original===

“And the ring, citizen?” Molé suggested presently.
The demagogue held up his left hand﻿—it was emaciated and disfigured by disease. A cheap-looking metal ring, set with a false stone, glistened upon the fourth finger.
“Take it off,” he said curtly.
The ring must have all along been too small for the bony hand of the once famous Court physician. Even now it appeared embedded in the flabby skin and refused to slide over the knuckle.
“The water will loosen it,” remarked Molé quietly.

===Chinese===

“那么戒指呢，公民？”过了一会儿莫莱提醒道。
那煽动家举起左手——枯瘦不堪，被病折磨得不成样子。第四根手指上一枚贱金属戒指闪着光，镶着一颗假宝石。
“取下来，”他没好气地说。
这戒指对这位当年大名鼎鼎的宫廷御医那把骨头手来说，想必一直嫌小。就是这会儿，它也像嵌在松弛的皮肉里，怎么也不肯滑过指节。
“水一泡就松了，”莫莱平静地说。

===Original===

Marat dipped his hand back into the water, and the other stood beside him, silent and stolid, his broad shoulders bent, his face naught but a mask, void and expressionless beneath its coating of grime.
One or two seconds went by. The air was heavy with steam and a medley of evil-smelling fumes, which hung in the close atmosphere of the narrow room. The sick man appeared to be drowsy, his head rolled over to one side, his eyes closed. He had evidently forgotten all about the ring.
A woman’s voice, shrill and peremptory, broke the silence which had become oppressive:
“Here, citizen Molé, I want you! There’s not a bit of wood chopped up for my fire, and how am I to make the coffee without firing, I should like to know?”

===Chinese===

马拉把手浸回水里。另一个站在旁边，沉默而木然，宽肩膀佝偻着，那张脸在污垢之下只是一张面具，空洞而无表情。
一两秒钟过去了。空气里满是水汽和种种恶臭的气味，沉甸甸地悬在这间窄屋的浊闷空气里。病人像是昏昏欲睡，头歪向一边，眼睛闭着。他显然把戒指的事忘了个干净。
一个女人的声音，尖利而专横，打破了那越来越叫人透不过气来的寂静：
“喂，莫莱公民，我要你过来！我的炉子边一块劈好的柴都没有，没有柴怎么煮咖啡，我倒要请问？”

===Original===

“The ring, citizen,” Molé urged gruffly.
Marat had been roused by the woman’s sharp voice. He cursed her for a noisy harridan; then he said fretfully:
“It will do presently﻿—when you are ready to start. I said nine o’clock﻿ ﻿… it is only four now. I am tired. Tell citizeness Evrard to bring me some hot coffee in an hour’s time.﻿ ﻿… You can go and fetch me the *Moniteur* now, and take back these proofs to citizen Dufour. You will find him at the Cordeliers, or else at the printing works.﻿ ﻿… Come back at nine o’clock.﻿ ﻿… I am tired now﻿ ﻿… too tired to tell you where to find the house which is off the Chemin de Pantin. Presently will do.﻿ ﻿…”
Even while he spoke he appeared to drop into a fitful sleep. His two hands were hidden under the sheet which covered the bath. Molé watched him in silence for a moment or two, then he turned on his heel and shuffled off through the anteroom into the kitchen beyond, where presently he sat down, squatting in an angle by the stove, and started with his usual stolidness to chop wood for the citizeness’ fire.
When this task was done, and he had received a chunk of sour bread for his reward from Jeannette Maréchal, the cook, he shuffled out of the place and into the street, to do his employer’s errands.

===Chinese===

“戒指，公民，”莫莱粗声催道。
马拉被那女人尖利的嗓门吵醒了。他骂了她一句聒噪的泼妇，随即烦躁地说：
“等会儿再给——等你动身的时候。我说的是九点……现在才四点。我累了。叫埃夫拉尔女公民过一个钟头给我送点热咖啡来。……你现在去给我取《导报》（Moniteur），把这些校样送回给迪富尔（Dufour）公民。他要么在科德利埃俱乐部，要么在印刷所。……九点钟回来。……我现在累了……累得没法告诉你潘坦路边上那所房子怎么找了。等会儿再说。……”
他话没说完，像是已经坠入了断断续续的瞌睡。他双手藏在盖着浴盆的布单下面。莫莱默默看了他一两分钟，转身拖着脚步穿过前厅进了里面的厨房，随即在一个墙角挨着炉子蹲下来，带着他惯常的木然，替女公民劈起生火的柴来。
等这活干完，他从厨娘让内特·马雷夏尔手里领到一块酸面包作赏钱，便拖着脚出了门，上街办主人的差事去了。

### IV / ### 四

===Original===

Paul Molé had been to the offices of the *Moniteur* and to the printing works of *L’Ami du Peuple*. He had seen the citizen Dufour at the Club and, presumably, had spent the rest of his time wandering idly about the streets of the quartier, for he did not return to the Rue des Cordeliers until nearly nine o’clock.
As soon as he came to the top of the street, he fell in with the crowd which had collected outside No. 30. With his habitual slouchy gait and the steady pressure of his powerful elbows, he pushed his way to the door, whilst gleaning whisperings and rumours on his way.
“The citizen Marat has been assassinated.”
“By a woman.”
“A mere girl.”
“A wench from Caen. Her name is Corday.”

===Chinese===

保罗·莫莱去了《导报》报馆，又去了《人民之友报》（L'Ami du Peuple）的印刷所。他在俱乐部见了迪富尔公民，余下的时间，想必都在街区街巷里闲荡，因为等他回到科德利埃街，已经快九点了。
他刚走到街口，就撞上了聚在 30 号门外的人群。他迈着惯常的懒散步子，用有力的胳膊肘不动声色地开路，挤向门口，一路还收罗着种种低语和风声。
“马拉公民遇刺了。”
“是个女人干的。”
“不过是个姑娘。”
“卡昂（Caen）来的一个妞儿。她姓科黛（Corday）。”

===Original===

“The people nearly tore her to pieces awhile ago.”
“She is as much as guillotined already.”
The latter remark went off with a loud guffaw and many a ribald joke.
Molé, despite his great height, succeeded in getting through unperceived. He was of no account, and he knew his way inside the house. It was full of people: journalists, gaffers, women and men﻿—the usual crowd that come to gape. The citizen Marat was a great personage. The Friend of the People. An Incorruptible, if ever there was one. Just look at the simplicity, almost the poverty, in which he lived! Only the aristos hated him, and the fat bourgeois who battened on the people. Citizen Marat had sent hundreds of them to the guillotine with a stroke of his pen or a denunciation from his fearless tongue.
Molé did not pause to listen to these comments. He pushed his way through the throng up the stairs, to his late employer’s lodgings on the first floor.

===Chinese===

“刚才老百姓差一点儿把她撕了。”
“她跟上了断头台也差不多了。”
这末一句话引来一阵哄堂大笑和许多下流的玩笑。
莫莱尽管个子高大，却做到了不引人注意地挤了进去。他是个无足轻重的人，屋里他又熟门熟路。屋里挤满了人：新闻记者、看热闹的老头、男人和女人——都是跑来瞪眼瞧稀奇的那路人。马拉公民是位大人物。人民之友。不折不扣的一个不可腐蚀者。瞧瞧他过的多么俭朴、简直清贫的日子！只有贵族佬恨他，还有那些吸食民脂民膏的肥资产阶级恨他。马拉公民大笔一挥，或是凭着那条无所畏惧的舌头告上一状，已把成百的这样的人送上了断头台。
莫莱没有停下来听这些议论。他从人堆里挤过去上了楼，到二楼他那位已故主人的住处去。

===Original===

The anteroom was crowded, so were the other rooms; but the greatest pressure was around the door immediately facing him, the one which gave on the bathroom. In the kitchen on his right, where awhile ago he had been chopping wood under a flood of abuse from Jeannette Maréchal, he caught sight of this woman, cowering by the hearth, her filthy apron thrown over her head, and crying﻿—yes! crying for the loathsome creature, who had expiated some of his abominable crimes at the hands of a poor, misguided girl, whom an infuriated mob was even now threatening to tear to pieces in its rage.
